## Global Data Watch

![](images/11c8d8d8bc33eb28599a0fa6c89b0f242df87bb0afa359a30669335ed51600b2.jpg)

A constructive 1H24 ending with some concerns; Business behavior key

Moderating inflation raises odds of Fed and ECB ease in September

● UK and French elections to highlight fiscal pressure points

● Next week: Global June PMIs (down), US payrolls (200k, urate 4%)

## The tortured economists department

We expected 1H24 to signal that the global expansion settled onto a firmer foundation as the base of global growth broadened to being less dependent on the US. This looks to have been realized as the US is tracking a moderation to a roughly 1.7%ar, while GDP growth elsewhere (ex. China) has accelerated to an abovepotential 2.3%ar (Figure 1). In addition to ending two years of US outperformance, 1H24 has delivered a revival in the expansion’s weakest links. Following last year’s stall, Western Europe is on track for a 1.5%ar 1H24 GDP gain. At the same time, a prolonged period of contraction in industry outside the Asian tech sector looks to have ended.

Alongside this constructive news, there is a new set of concerns linked to recent swings in momentum and sentiment. Despite solid overall first half performance, global growth looks have slowed sharply this quarter as the EM downshifted following a strong start to the year. Combined with the news that US imports are rebounding—pointing to a close to 1%ar 1H24 US net trade drag—it is not clear how much dependence on US demand has been reduced. Related to this news, the recent pickup in global manufacturing—factory output ex. China expanded at a 5.2%ar in the three months through April—was accompanied by a stall in global retail spending over this period. Global service spending appears to be still expanding at a solid pace, but the failure of sentiment and spending to lift in the face of a substantial boost to purchasing power from falling inflation and elevated wage gains is disappointing (Figure 2).

In assessing these concerns our focus is on business sector behavior. Profit margins were pushed to record high levels over 2021-22 by policy supports and surging

nominal GDP growth. Since that time, earnings have slowed but our tracker of DM margins has remained stable. Meanwhile, firms have absorbed a sharp rise in borrowing costs that has not passed through to a tightening in broader financial conditions. The business response to these developments has been a turn toward caution on capital outlays while hiring has continued at a rapid pace (Figure 3).

A central pillar of our 2H24 global forecast is that firms remain in expansion mode with global employment gains settling at about a 1%ar and capex rising modestly. In this regard, our capex nowcaster and the Asian tech sector signal that business spending has picked up since the start of the year. Labor demand indicators have moderated from their 2021-23 highs but still point to solid employment gains as we move through 1H24. Next week’s US June employment report is expected to deliver a firm 200k rise in jobs.

Figure 3: Business spending
![](images/c3fc2d2bc49a0d4f2ae74f252aec209e11bd986248a538e4870cf384104224db.jpg)

With global CPI inflation moderating this quarter and labor cost gains still elevated, we maintain household spending will rebound in 2H24. Our mantra has been: Give them jobs and they will spend. With this in mind, we are encouraged by several reports pointing to a strong increase in global goods consumption in May. We are also mindful of the ongoing recovery in service sector spending, which is always more difficult to track but has underlain a large part of the health of the consumer over the past year. Anecdotes of record airline travel heading into this summer suggest service spending on experiences remains a large source of growth.

## Tech upturn returns to non-China Asia

Asia industrial production (ex. China) contracted at the start of this year as last year’s tech sector boom appeared to be fading. The recent news from the region shows renewed strength in tech boosting activity again (Figure 4). This is most evident in Taiwan where May IP surged alongside a jump in tech export orders. Korea’s IP dipped in May due to a fall in non-tech production. While its tech IP is lagging the regional rebound, an 18.8% jump in May semiconductor shipments points to a fall in inventories that will spur production in the coming months. Japan’s IP has been volatile amid temporary suspensions of auto output. The rebound in May IP led by auto production is set to be followed by a contraction around midyear. But the upswing in Japan’s tech sector will likely continue as it benefits from expanded production capacity in integrated circuits. Overall, the indicators that solid external demand seems to be feeding the tech upturn bode positively for global capex and regional GDP growth.

## The ECB and Fed: See you in September

Both the ECB and the Fed point to September as the next meeting in which rates may be cut. While we forecast the ECB to ease and the Fed to remain on hold until November, both decisions are highly dependent on incoming releases. Signs that the first quarter inflation rebound has receded bolster confidence in ECB and Fed forecasts for a slide toward target next year. However, it will take further positive inflation readings to cement the case for a September ease. Euro area national reports are thus far in line with our projection for core HICP to rise 0.24%m/m, sa in next week’s June flash report. With a weaker June PMI and national surveys increasing near-term growth nervousness, this relatively neutral inflation signal will probably be sufficient to keep a September easing on track.

For the Fed, this week delivered small offsetting surprises on the monthly profile of core PCE inflation. With core PCE inflation rising at a 3.5%ar thus far this year, the FOMC will likely need consistent low readings in the three CPI reports leading to the September meeting in order to ease. As we are not expecting weak job gains, signs of better balance in the labor market can also boost the case for a September ease. In this regard, next week’s JOLTS report, the payroll report details, and the upcoming 2Q24 ECI will provide important information for Fed guidance at the July meeting.

## The Riksbank guides to an August ease

Despite the upside surprise in May core inflation, Sweden’s Riksbank delivered dovish forward guidance this week.

The Board thus decided to look through services prices boosted by Swift-flation and downplayed GDP revisions that removed last year’s contraction. We continue to expect three cuts this year but have responded to their signal by moving forward our next cut to August (previously September).

## Here come European elections

France and the UK head to the polls next week, with political changes anticipated. In France, a three-way split between the far-right RN, the Left alliance, and Macron’s Ensemble seems likely in the first round. Translating this outcome into an end result will be difficult, but RN looks likely to win without receiving an absolute majority. This outcome creates little clarity about how a government can be formed and how a 2025 budget can be passed. In terms of substance, however, RN has dropped many costly measures from its platform and has said that it would broadly respect the EU’s fiscal rules. A risk of fiscal slippage will likely remain in place.

In the UK General Election, Labor is expected to win with a large majority. The remaining questions are “how large” and if the Reform party gains a higher share of the national vote than the Conservative party (the Conservative party will still gain significantly more seats than the Reform party reflecting the “first past the post system”). Given a Labor majority has been discounted by markets for a while, and they have made no substantive new policy announcements during the campaign, we do not expect the election result to be consequential for markets. On the fiscal side, the incoming government will be boxed in given limited fiscal headroom, significant spending needs, and pre-existing commitments to not increase taxes in many of the largest revenue raising areas.

## CEE delivers cautious easing

Against a backdrop of sticky inflation progress and cautious DM central banks, we have expected CEE central banks to slow the pace of easing. While Hungary’s NBH followed this approach, Czechia’s CNB surprised with a fourth consecutive 50bp cut. The dovish action was, however, tempered by hawkish guidance as Governor Michl noted that the easing cycle could end in restrictive territory, and that the next move was likely to be smaller or a pause. Next week, we look for the National Bank of Romania (NBR) to deliver a first rate cut in response to recent downside inflation surprises. Poland’s NBP, meanwhile, is likely to remain on hold as the preliminary June CPI showed core inflation still running at the upper end of its target band.

## China: no fireworks in July

China’s growth remains on a softening trend into mid-year as policy adjustments since May have been gradual and insufficient to bolster domestic demand, especially in the flailing housing sector. Next month’s Politburo meeting offers an opportunity to reset near-term policy supports, but a seachange is unlikely. While housing policy adjustment has received a stronger endorsement from top policymakers recently, we see downside risks to our forecast that growth will return to a trend-like 4.5%ar in 2H24. Ahead of the Politburo meeting, the 3rd Plenum of the 20th CPC Party Congress on July 15-18 will address the structural reform agenda affecting China’s longer-term growth outlook. We expect it to reiterate the priorities laid out in the 2017 Party Congress, including broadening the base of growth, a more balanced policy mix, and progress on key reform areas (e.g., rural land reform, fiscal reform, and a unified national market).

## Divergent CB responses in LatAm

Although Latin American central banks are typically sensitive to currency weakening, we expect them to respond differently to the most recent sell-off. The BCB and Banxico have turned more cautious, but there is a distinct risk the BCB could hike rates this year in response to a deteriorating inflation outlook. While pushing back on market pricing of hikes for now, COPOM’s recent communication also looks consistent with a central bank that is prepared to tighten quickly if needed, even as our base case sees it on hold. Banxico faces a more complex trade-off as it balances benign core inflation and softer GDP growth against currency weakness in the wake of the elections. A more dovish statement prompted us to pull forward the next cut by a month to August but with a subsequent move only in December. The Andeans should be less affected and push ahead with more easing. Colombia’s Ban-Rep this week maintained its 50bp easing pace amid falling inflation, high rate buffers, and recent fiscal adjustment. If our below-consensus inflation forecast materializes, the pace of easing will likely speed up in the last three meetings of the year.

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our forecast for 2Q24 global real GDP growth was unchanged this week at 2.2%ar (Figure 1). Our top-down global nowcaster similarly moved sideways at 2.3%ar, suggesting balanced risks to global growth (Table 2).

About 40% of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in just over 60% of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests an acceleration in capex growth from 2.4%ar in 1Q24 to 3.2%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

We continue to see the fundamentals in place for a final demand-led lift in global industry, but the latest signals are mixed: global export volumes rose in April, but nominal May exports look soft and the June flash DM PMIs disappointed. Meanwhile, global core CPI rose 0.25% in May, leaving the three-month trend elevated at 3.4%ar.

## US: Economic policy implications of the election

The upcoming elections could have significant consequences for the macroeconomy next year and beyond

Trade, immigration, industrial, and competition policies will be shaped by the presidential election

• Winners of the presidential and congressional elections will immediately be confronted by fiscal challenges

There’s no hiding from it any longer, this is an election year. It still appears that Biden and Trump will face off in a presidential rematch, with the Senate likely flipping to the Republicans and the House flipping to the Democrats. In this note we look at which policies could change in ways that have macroeconomic significance - in the coming weeks we will be discussing these issues in greater detail, but what follows is a high-level discussion of the key economic policy issues.

## Trade: Trump promises more tariffs

While the Biden administration hasn’t been a vocal advocate of free trade—leaving intact the Trump tariffs on Chinese imports and recently selectively increasing tariffs further on some Chinese products—a second Trump administration would be much more protectionist than a second Biden administration. Among other measures, Trump has mentioned two possible policies: a 60% tariff on all Chinese imports, and a 10% universal tariff on all imports.

The Trump 1.0 tariffs increased the trade-weighted average tariff rate from around 1.5% to just over 3%, raising an additional \$60 billion in customs revenue. If this all fell on consumers it would have raised the basket of consumer prices by around 0.3%. Evidence from that period suggests that there was less than complete pass-through to consumer prices. In other words, retailers and other businesses swallowed some of the higher tariffs. On the other hand, there is also evidence that domestic producers used some of the increase in market power to push through higher prices. So we think 0.3% is a reasonable estimate of the price level effect of those tariffs.

Following a similar reasoning, a 60% tariff on all Chinese imports—about a 48%-point increase—would statically raise just over \$200 billion, raising the price level 1.1%. A similar calculation for a 10% universal tariff yields \$280 billion, or about 1.5% on the price level. Going from a static to a dynamic calculation would likely change the results for the Chinese tariff more dramatically, as it may be easier to substitute away from China to other low-cost producers than to substitute away from imported products altogether.

In standard models the economic growth impact of tariffs is surprisingly small—even Paul Krugman has called this the “dirty little secret of trade theory.” However, announcements around the Trump 1.0 tariffs had large and adverse effects on the stock market. This disconnect may reflect that trade models don’t capture intangibles like policy uncertainty. In any case, significant uncertainty attends to estimates of the impact on growth.

Finally, it’s worth highlighting that the president has broad authority to craft trade policy without Congressional involvement, as discussed in our recent webinar with Jamieson Greer, former chief of staff to Trump’s USTR Robert Lighthizer (replay link here).

## Fiscal: Biden would limit TCJA extension

More so than other issues we cover here, the shape of fiscal policy will depend on both the presidential and Congressional elections. The winners of those elections will be immediately faced with the expiration of the most important personal tax provisions of the 2017 Tax Cut and Jobs Act (TCJA) at the end of 2025. This includes lower tax rates, expanded child tax credit, an increased standard deduction (partly to compensate for eliminating personal and dependent exemptions), increased estate tax exemption, and a deduction for passthrough income. If all these features of the TCJA were allowed to expire as currently written in law, tax receipts are estimated to increase by just under \$400 billion per year, or around 1.3% of GDP.

President Biden has stated his intention to effectively extend the TCJA provisions for those making under \$400,000 but to revert to the older, higher rates for those with incomes above that limit. Biden’s plan also involves increasing the base for the net investment income tax and the additional Medicare tax for those making over \$400,000. On the business side, Biden has proposed raising the corporate rate from 21% to 28%, increasing the GILTI tax rate to 21%, and miscellaneous other revenue increases. After accounting for revenue lost from an expanded child tax credit and other tax credits, the Biden plan would raise around \$150 billion per year, or about a half percent of GDP.

The Trump campaign has been less specific on their fiscal plans. Trump has said he favors a “middle class, upper class, lower class, business class big tax cut.” Presumably he and the Republicans in Congress would extend all provisions of the TCJA. If Trump needed to compromise with a Democratic-controlled House, it’s possible that the corporate tax rate could move higher. And as alluded to earlier, any revenue raised from higher tariffs would be seen by a Trump White House as funding the TCJA extension or expansion.

## Immigration: Trump vows to slow inflows

The differences between the two presidential candidates on immigration are quite stark. While President Biden recently tightened up border entry rules, the immigration numbers have remained firm through May (Figure 1). In contrast, former President Trump has vowed to shut the southwest border and begin large-scale deportations of unauthorized immigrants already in the country.

![](images/c1bf7496c7bc19632f3bedff1f0e3e4b407130a6b866477d918639ff77904100.jpg)

Legal experts are divided on whether these large-scale deportations would be allowed by the courts. But there is little doubt that the president has broad authority to limit immigration, and that a second Trump administration would devote significant resources to stopping border crossings.

Earlier in the year we wrote about the various ways higher immigration is affecting the economic data (link here). To briefly recap some of our key findings, we believe one of the most important impacts is to raise trend, or breakeven, monthly job growth from a ballpark of around 100,000 to around 200,000. This also helps explain why the unemployment rate has drifted higher while job growth has remained brisk. The associated growth in household formation has likely also kept housing vacancy rates low even while housing completions have been solid.

The inflationary impact is heavily debated. Immigrants add to both labor supply and consumer demand, though some of that demand may leak out of the country via remittances to home countries. So the net effect may be disinflationary, but we think it is modest and close to a wash. Under a second Trump administration we would expect that many of these economic effects of elevated immigration would go in reverse, as immigration flows would downshift markedly.

## Industrial policy: Modest green transition risks

By some counts, the IRA and CHIPS Act have stimulated close to one-half trillion dollars in announced investments in semiconductor and clean tech manufacturing facilities. This has been a crucial support to business investment outlays in what would otherwise be a challenging capex environment.

Opposition to the green transition by Trump and some other Republicans could pose a risk to parts of this spending. However, our colleagues in energy research believe this is a manageable risk for two reasons (link here). First, a full repeal of the IRA would require a red sweep, which currently isn’t believed to be the most likely outcome. In the absence of such a red sweep, administrative actions by a Trump White House could slow DOE lending and lead to a downsizing of some subsidies, but much of the IRA support would continue. Second, under any electoral outcome, the majority of IRA and CHIPS Act spending is occurring in traditionally Republican, southern, and rural counties. So under even a red sweep, Republicans may soften their opposition to those acts.

Another point our colleagues make is that while Republican policies would challenge green tech capex, they are supportive of fossil fuel capex. Global supply-demand factors will be the prime mover of domestic exploration and production activity, but at the margin, actions like rolling back some of the Biden administration EPA rules should be helpful for spending in this sector.

## Anti-trust: Trump may be more lenient

The Biden administration has taken an aggressive approach to antitrust enforcement. Google, Meta, and NAR are some of the higher-profile names that have come under the scrutiny of the DOJ and FTC. A second Trump administration would likely take a more lenient approach to enforcement of the relevant statutes. Even so, some conservatives are becoming more receptive to a vigorous competition policy, earning the name “Khan-servatives” in reference to current FTC chair Lina Khan. Even the Heritage Foundation’s Project 2025— sometimes seen as a blueprint for a second Trump administration—is amenable to antitrust enforcement actions against Big Tech (link here).

Is this a macroeconomic issue? Maybe not for the contour of the business cycle, but many economists believe increasing industrial concentration has long-run macroeconomic impacts, including increased profit margins and a lower labor share of income. But even after more than three years of the Biden administration, the labor share remains near all-time lows, indicating that these industrial trends won’t turn on a dime with a change in the White House.

## EU elections: centrist majority but French wildcard

Broad centrist coalition intact in European Parliament despite far right gaining

• Some variation across countries: in Scandinavia, leftleaning and green parties did better than expected

Direction of EU policy unlikely to change much given ongoing pivot from green to competitiveness policies

Far right gains, especially in France, likely to make bar for additional EU-level instruments higher

• Focus on France and potential challenge to the new EU fiscal rules

Polls ahead of the EU election had suggested a shift to the political right (see our preview). While this materialized it was not uniform across countries. Table 1 recaps the political groupings that MEPs align themselves with.

Table 1: Political groupings for EP election
<table><tr><td>Name</td><td>Alignment</td><td>Lead Candidate</td></tr><tr><td>EPP - European People&#x27;s Party</td><td>Centre-right</td><td>von der Leyen</td></tr><tr><td>S&amp;D - Alliance of Socialists, Democrats</td><td>Centre-left</td><td>Schmitt</td></tr><tr><td>Renew Europe</td><td>Liberal</td><td>Hayer (tentative)</td></tr><tr><td>Greens/EFA - Greens/European</td><td>Green</td><td>Eikhout, Reintke,</td></tr><tr><td>Free Alliance ECR - European</td><td></td><td>Rossberg, Romeva</td></tr><tr><td>Conservatives/Reformists</td><td>Right/Eurosceptic</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>ID - Identity and Democracy</td><td>Right/Eurosceptic</td><td>Vistisen</td></tr><tr><td>The Left</td><td>Left</td><td>Baier</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan

The ECR and ID groupings did pick up votes, but not quite as much as expected. Indeed, the EPP increased its share of seats (Figure 1) versus expectations of a slight decline.

Figure 1: EP election seats
![](images/f5821c567b5f947ecdd6f106f065271973d0292efa1a7f8cd3f4100154835564.jpg)
Source: EC., J.P. Morgan. \*Outgoing parliment (as of June 2024)

This implies the existing centrist coalition of EPP, S&D, and Renew should have clear majority of seats (currently above

55 percent of the vote). The final tally, however, will not be known until July 4, as alliances to the various groupings can shift (e.g., Renew, which includes Macron’s Renaissance MEPs, has lost some seats over the two weeks).

As is often the case in the EU, there were some important country-level differences. Le Pen’s National Rally (under the ID grouping) doing very well in France was not unexpected; Macron calling early national lower house elections as a result was unexpected. In Germany, the centre-right party (CDU/CSU) came out on top (under the EPP grouping), as expected. The governing coalition (SPD, the Greens, FDP) were expecting losses, but perhaps not on the scale experienced, with the far right AfD gaining the second largest vote share. In Scandinavia, left-leaning and Green parties did better than expected. For example, in Finland, the Left Alliance surged from one seat to three. And this is in the context of Finland having formed a governing coalition in June 2023 considered by many to be the most right wing in its history. In Denmark, the results were even more striking: the Green alliance won 20 percent of the seats.

## Limited implications for EU policy

As we noted in our election preview, these results in themselves should not lead to major changes in the general direction of policy at the EU level. There was already a modest shift from green to competitiveness policies taking place. This reflected renewed challenges from the US and China, and expectations of a political shift to the right following the trends over the last year in national and state elections in Germany, the Netherlands, Portugal, and Spain.

And while not guaranteed, given the performance of the EPP in the elections, Ursula Von der Leyen remains the clear favourite for the Commission presidency. A second term for her should imply a significant degree of continuity in the Commission’s agenda. If she is informally approved at an EU leaders’ meeting later this week, then she could be potentially formally voted in for a second term by the European Parliament as early as mid-July. Votes on Commissioners would follow after that, and the new Commission could potentially be fully operational before end-2024, including having published its’ official 5-year work program.

In terms of specific focus points, certainly making further progress on the capital markets union (CMU) to leverage private capital has increasingly been brought up in political discussions and the subject of major recent reports by Enrica Letta and the French Treasury. Mario Draghi, both former president of the ECB and prime minister of Italy, is expected to publish a report on European competitiveness in early July, including calling for an industrial strategy for Europe. The report is still very much seen as a key plank of the Commission’s next 5-year work program.

However, as we also noted in our preview, the disagreements amongst member states that have stymied CMU progress before are still very prevalent (e.g., on harmonization on insolvency regimes and withholding taxes). So rapid progress will likely be hard to deliver, especially without further progress on banking union. Moreover, making industrial policies consistent with state aid rules will be far from easy, especially given extremely different views amongst EU member states regarding reform of the latter.

Notwithstanding clear threats to EU competitiveness, it’s hard to see significant and rapid action in the near term given continued divisions among member states. Moreover, if NR does end up forming a government in France, this will likely reduce the push for further EU-level instruments and CMU from a key stakeholder. Macron has been a big supporter of further EU integration, whereas an NR government is likely to be much more circumspect. We still hold the view that the U.S. election could be more consequential for the creation of further EU-level support, particularly when it comes to defence spending.

## Focus on France and EU Fiscal Rules

The call for early lower house elections in France was a big shock to the system, as evidenced in the dramatic widening of the 10-year OAT-Bund spread (it still remains above 80bps— Figure 2).

Figure 2: Euro sovereign spreads (relative to 10 year bund) both bps
![](images/88732b80c3c466b745fd31c72e865c2edc0976d3a567128db69a9befc3b9f94f.jpg)

Ahead of the first round of voting on June 30, the situation remains fluid, although our baseline is for Le Pen’s National Rally (NR) to end up with some kind of majority. Whether they would accept to govern with a relative majority remains to be seen, so political uncertainty could well remain over the summer.

As we noted elsewhere, the greater concern is about the impact of the current French political crisis on the potential for further fiscal slippage—as we saw in 2023—rather than the impact of the current rise in sovereign spreads on interest payments. The latter has not led to a significant rise in sovereign bond rates. Indeed, rates have declined in Italy and Spain since the EU elections took place (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Euro sovereign yields
![](images/50941d95dc48f850108c9599c78c211eb8fcddd9ab3ef17adfc77ef5bb755c8e.jpg)

The 2025 draft budgets that are due in the Autumn were always going to be a key focal point given new fiscal rules are in place and that much of the low hanging fruit of deficit reduction has been picked already (pandemic and energy price support rolling off). Essentially, the French early lower house elections have brought forward attention on fiscal paths going forward. And, as expected, the Commission announced last Wednesday that excessive deficit procedures (EDPs) were being recommended for France and Italy, along with 5 other countries.

Overall, some further fiscal slippage in France seems likely but it is hard to gauge its likely magnitude at this stage, especially given uncertainty about the application of the new EU fiscal rules. Certainly, there is a risk that the minimum structural primary adjustment required under an EDP (0.5 percent of GDP) could be challenged by either an NR or NFP (the left wing alliance) government. So the draft 2025 Budget and medium-term fiscal plan, both due in the autumn, will be under the market microscope. This is also true for Italy given it is also being proposed for an EDP and has a debt level of close to 140 percent of GDP.

bp contribution to %m/m headline CPI, sa

## Mexico: Inflation slows toward a higher average

Inflation has been subject to shocks, structural and cyclical, short- and long-term

• As some recede, a clearer picture of underlying inflation emerges

• Services CPI gains are easing, but appear to have shifted structurally higher

• Goods CPI contained on (until now) strong MXN, which seems too strong vs. fundamentals

CPI inflation has seen gyrations similar to those across the globe: headline and core inflation accelerated sharply in the immediate aftermath of the pandemic, followed by a prolonged period of high but slowing inflation. Inflation has gone a long way toward normalizing, but still remains north of 4% (Banxico’s target is 3%). Here we focus on core inflation, as it provides a better signal on underlying inflation trends. In our view the worst for core inflation is past and it should continue to moderate ahead. But it is likely that it averages above pre-pandemic levels. We think core inflation is likely to remain around 4% rather than between 3% and 3.5%, as used to be the norm.

## Core goods lead the slowing (everywhere)

Core inflation has slowed markedly in the past several quarters, and is currently sitting just above the upper bound of Banxico’s target range (4%). As elsewhere, goods disinflation has been the main driver of the shift lower, while services inflation remains relatively high (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Core goods and services
![](images/3d08b477ffd4025efe3d60a236811e163b92cbfe798066d8bb334a5e40ff1765.jpg)

## Services CPI seems to be yielding

The last handful of inflation prints suggest core inflation will continue to slow. While annual core inflation has been easing only marginally, the composition of core inflation has been more suggestive of further disinflation, supporting the recent downward revision to our end 2024 core inflation forecast

(3.7%). Services inflation may be starting to slow more noticeably after a long spell of stickiness at very high rates. Goods prices remain in check, partly reflecting a strong currency, and the latest data suggest they are still increasing at a below-normal rate. The welcome development is that core services seems to be finally easing (Figure 2).

![](images/579e0511c99611683535708f7da883d526e3bdc34788ea05a34397dcc2c36ba1.jpg)

Overall services has been a bit softer than we expected despite firm housing prices and large gains in some tourism categories. However, when we strip out these categories and look at non-tourism services (which also exclude education), prices are finally cooling. This is a significant change from the dynamics witnessed earlier in the year. Non-tourism price gains, which are least subject to swings related to unusual seasonality (leap-year, Easter), had been on a modest downtrend between Feb-Apr, but remained notably high (roughly 0.57%m/m, sa on average), almost at double their pre-pandemic norm (0.3%). By contrast, non-tourism services slowed markedly in May and likely even further in June. By our estimates non-tourism services prices increased just 0.18%m/m, sa in June, well below the norm, and the smallest increase since March 2022. Restaurants, which account for about 50% of non-tourism services, have been largely behind the slowdown (Table 1).

Furthermore, save for a few exceptions, most relevant items within non-tourism services are falling back to their pre-pan-

% of national income, 4q rolling average

demic norm, or even running below it. In fact, the 1H June rate of change in the non-tourism services aggregate moved below its pre-pandemic mean.

Figure 3: Z-score for Other Services CPI components
![](images/608f7d7904818b8c18466e78f1c4ba59f04728e83c6d20fbad88445c80af6f81.jpg)
Source: INEGI. CPI weights in parentheses. \*Five-year pre-pandemic average for June 1H

## Don’t expect the pre-pandemic average

The recent data suggest core inflation will moderate ahead, as transitory sources of pressure on the services front appear to be ebbing more notably and a strong currency helps contain core goods prices. Our forecast for core inflation at 3.7% by year-end reflects our expectation that recent slower rates of change in core prices will hold. Even if the recent peso depreciation proves permanent, firms very likely hedged their dollar exposure during the lengthy spell of hefty MXN strength, thus limiting passthrough in the short term. Gradually cooler domestic demand growth also suggests softer inflation in the short term. That said, there are reasons to think that Mexico’s average core rate of inflation for the medium term has shifted higher, as reflected in our forecast for 2025, which has it near 4%.

Figure 4: Labor share in national income
![](images/14f671ab371a0903f4ef17dc0feeef2f92302044dc38c1229ec3c6e429de8019.jpg)

Unlike elsewhere, Mexico’s divergence in core goods and services disinflation is being exacerbated by idiosyncratic forces, some of which appear clearly structural, and others that may or may not be. The clearest structural force is the minimum wage policy enacted since 2017 (see here). This policy has raised the share of labor in national income, which has reversed a good part of the decline seen in the past couple of decades, and seems set to continue rising (Figure 4). The

higher labor income share in national income has combined with very robust household balance sheets—financial wealth surged about 10%-pts of GDP between 2019 and 2022. This in turn has translated into very strong consumption growth on a trend basis in the past several years, but most evident during the current expansion (Figure 5).

![](images/5b8b3d9ca1e442a94318b0323d76d32c897e262e2269dc41e78743e53a26259f.jpg)

Mexico’s core goods inflation has slowed thanks to common global forces such as easing supply chain constraints and commodity prices. But the slowing has also come from the very large appreciation in the currency seen after the sell-off in the immediate aftermath of the pandemic. Core goods price sequential gains have fallen clearly below their pre-pandemic norm, and specifically driven by nonfood goods prices, the most sensitive to changes in the exchange (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Wage- and FX-related inflation
![](images/1b4c90548d3712486de070e5b0ba597a70fdebcfb1fe65baf53180a61ac4aabd.jpg)
Source: Banxico and J.P. Morgan. Pre-policy refers prior to min. wage hikes (2011-17). \*Pre-pandemic (2011-19) average for FX related inflation.

Unlike the structural shift brought about by wage policies, it is not clear whether the appreciation of the Mexican peso should be seen as permanent. Arguments for peso strength include a relatively contained debt burden, a strong current account, and prospects for an increase in investment and FDI due to supply chain relocation. But Banxico’s extremely high ex-ante real policy rate has also played a significant role in strengthening the currency.

In our view the real exchange rate appears overvalued relative to fundamentals, notwithstanding its recent correction. Relative productivity between Mexico and the US provides a guideline of the fair real relative purchasing power of the

labor force (Figure 7), and this suggests a big disconnect visà-vis the real exchange rate has developed over the last couple of years.

Figure 7: MX/US relative productivity and REER
![](images/31e67637f46aee47412abe35f275582b45d155244156d90550662adb02dfce09.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan with data from Banxico and BLS

There are other signals of excessive currency strength, with the jaw-dropping surge in imported goods consumption being the most conspicuous (Figure 8). A re-pricing of the Mexican peso that brings it closer to its fair fundamental level would also bring about higher inflation, though this would be transitory.

Figure 8: Real imported goods consumption Index, sa (2018=100)
![](images/f623cad42cf4f391c1ddbb3245829d90a534178ce37a21374c41e99aef39673e.jpg)
Source: INEGI

## Short-term relief, higher equilibrium

The analysis above suggests that some headwinds keeping core inflation from declining are ebbing, particularly those related to non-tourism services prices. At the same time, the strength of the currency should continue to help contain core goods prices, notwithstanding the recent depreciation. However, looking ahead, we think price formation has changed in a structural way: policies to boost wages, which will be here to stay at least for a while, are likely to keep services (domestic demand) and wage-related (from a cost perspective, supply) inflation higher over the next several years compared to pre-pandemic. We also think it unlikely that the currency acts as such a meaningful source of disinflation ahead, which would build on this structural shift higher. Hence, while we think the short-term inflation outlook has improved, we also posit that the new “equilibrium” rate of core inflation will be closer 4% than between 3% and 3.5% as was the case before.

## Argentina: The flipside of BCRA healing

The Milei administration has advanced promptly in reducing the consolidated public sector deficit

• The flipside has been the increase in the consolidated public sector debt stock

The delta in debt issuance increases Treasury’s future financing needs...

• ...requiring a higher structural primary surplus, as well as positive real rates ahead

A major macroeconomic imbalance was present as the Milei administration took office, namely a ballooning consolidated public sector deficit (Treasury and central bank), running at 13% of GDP (Figure 1). Levels like these were only observed prior to hyperinflation episodes in the past. On the Treasury side, the administration decided to tackle this via imposing a front-loaded fiscal adjustment, resorting to the debasement in real terms of some expenditures, as well as the increase of distortionary taxes (as PAIS tax). On the monetary side, authorities were concerned by the potential inability to prevent an inflationary spiral only via price adjustment. Hence the authorities maintained the capital controls cobweb and imposed a three-pronged strategy relying on negative real interest rates, issuance of central bank liabilities in the form of Bopreal, and a migration from short term local currency remunerated central bank liabilities to Treasury paper.

Figure 1: Consolidated public sector deficit % of GDP
![](images/89e19a129dcfd37a16272404ddcd339ade18de59ade5e176dae567101be17792.jpg)
The administration has reduced the deficit to near 6% of GDP in our estimates (for the last 12 months through May). That implies a close to 7%-pt of GDP fiscal consolidation in just five months. About 40% of that decline came from a reduction in the Treasury fiscal deficit, while the remaining 60% came from a decline in BCRA’s quasi-fiscal costs (interest on remunerated liabilities).

This sharp decline in the consolidated public sector fiscal imbalance has, in our view, erased the specter of a looming hyperinflation crisis and was a necessary condition for economic stabilization. Admittedly, other conditions are required to consolidate the regime change, among them the liberalization of financial flows, or removal of capital controls (see note).

Yet, as highlighted here, the ongoing correction of the central bank balance sheet is not absent of macro costs. The flipside of the reduction in BCRA’s quasi-fiscal deficit has been the increase in the Treasury public sector debt stock (measured at the parallel FX), after forcing the migration from BCRA remunerated liabilities into Treasury paper. In other words, the Treasury has capitalized the central bank, taking advantage of both the public sector savings deployed YTD, as well as the capital controls in place. Moreover, this arrangement allowed the authorities to drive real interest rates deep into negative territory, a policy that has already run its course.

A related point worth stressing is that, in conjunction with the capitalization of the central bank, the Treasury has also capitalized debt interests service. Indeed, note that the bulk of the YTD paper issued by the Treasury in pesos is zero-coupon. Thus, the delta in debt issuance doesn’t have an impact on Treasury’s cash interest bill (above the line, component of the headline fiscal balance). Instead it increases the Treasury’s future financing needs, so accounts “below the line”. This strategy will demand a higher structural primary surplus to stabilize the overall consolidated debt ahead, as well as positive real rates.

## Zooming into Treasury peso debt stock

As of June 2024, the Treasury peso debt stock reached ARS165tn on our estimates (US\$122bn equivalent at parallel FX), out of which we estimate 40% is in the hands of the private sector. This compares to US\$82bn equivalent at parallel FX by Dec-23 or US\$58bn when compared to Nov-23 predevaluation levels (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Peso debt stock evolution
![](images/4f3e041150ac64d836bc615ced769ede610282ace11959552951df0719ceb3ed.jpg)
By instrument type, inflation/FX adjusted linked debt continues to represents the bulk of the stock at 87% of the aggre-

gate. That said, the increase in the participation of fixed-rate instruments to 13% of the aggregate, when compared to just 4.3% by Dec-23 (Figure 3) is a positive development, in our view. On the other side, banks’ exposure to the Treasury has increased to 36% of assets, from 20% back in Nov-23 (or 47% including BCRA, from 45% back in Nov-23).

Figure 3: Peso debt stock breakdown
![](images/ab4e913cb832efba95b4dcc174c8cff2b1a46c77533fa0da171e79f6d793a472.jpg)
Source: MinFin and J.P. Morgan

The average duration of the peso debt has increased to 2.0 years from an estimated 1.4 at the end of last year. While the Treasury has been actively issuing in the short part of the curve lowering duration of instruments in hands of the private sector, the public sector extended duration through zero-coupon inflation-linked (Boncer) bonds.

## Mind contingents...

While not included in the local currency debt stock, it’s worth noting that Treasury paper for about ARS19tn (4% of GDP) has put options. Such optionality would have monetary implications if exercised. In fact, YTD about ARS 5.5tn has been exercised. On our estimates, the stock of puts is equivalent to 110.6% of the base money by June. About 24% of the stock matures in the second half of the year, while 50% matures in 2026-27. President Milei has emphasized that tackling the sizeable stock of put options is a pre-condition for removal of capital controls.

## ..and mind the wall

In terms of the Treasury debt maturity profile, around 25% of the stock now matures through year-end (Figure 4). Importantly, 90% of the maturing debt is in the hands of the private sector (Figure 5). Meanwhile, another 22% matures in 1H25, of which around half is privately held. The remainder matures in more than one year. In all, about 11% of GDP matures in the next 12 months using our annual 2024 GDP estimate (8% of GDP with the private sector). While challenging, the concentration of maturities in the short term is mitigated by the liquidity cushion built by the government, amounting to ARS13.5bn (2.5% of GDP). That said, the growing size of the peso debt stock raises rollover risks ahead, particularly in light of the uncertainty regarding the FX and monetary regime that will follow ahead.

Figure 4: Local currency debt amortizations by instrument
![](images/d08d235e7a68b47221563c48b4d628e49a07c1d2b4b375704e05054b039c29a7.jpg)

Figure 5: Local currency debt amortizations by secto
![](images/e2bda0c8ef349b6872e278b7c7fc98832172bc3c1cc9ec5a9b2d5bb7d0d5ee84.jpg)
Source: MinFin and J.P. Morgan

## Treasury financing uses and sources

We revise Treasury’s financial uses and sources for the remainder of the year and for full-year 2025. Granted, uncertainty is still heightened in terms of the FX and monetary regime the administration aims to transition into, as well as its timing. In addition, legislative action would have a say on the ability of the administration to maintain a fiscal balance ahead.

Against this backdrop, it’s worth clarifying some of the assumptions we rely upon. For the remainder of this year, we assume capital controls are gradually eased from 3Q24, still allowing for 100% of peso debt rollover (vs. 152% YTD). Moreover, we also assume that the government prints a 0% primary deficit through Jun-December 2024. On these assumptions, the administration would close the year with excess net financing for ARS5.2bn or 0.9% of GDP, serving as a liquidity cushion into next year.

The picture looks more challenging in 2025 amid increases in both peso and USD financing needs. On the peso side, we assume that 70% of the debt rolled over from 2024 matures next year, which brings the local currency debt maturities in full-year 2025 up to 11% of GDP (5.7% of GDP with the pri vate sector). Meanwhile, on the foreign currency side, debt service amounts to US\$17bn, of which US\$8.8bn represents amortizations and US\$8.2bn interest payments. Regarding amortizations, the obligations are for US\$5.8bn in market

Figure 6: External amortizations

debt, and another US\$3bn to IFIs (note there are no principal payments to the IMF in 2025, Figures 6 and 7). We are not including US\$2.3bn in debt service in 2025 related to Bopreal, in the hands of BCRA.

![](images/48cf38110bfb416ae9f10de39233f86f4bc13b82a5a550065044b65d5805218a.jpg)

Figure 7: External debt service (capital + interest)
![](images/e50954cc315eb46db5bab7a424035c553dbdfe7365eebb55a63c95673eaba1c1.jpg)

In this scenario, the authorities may launch a local debt exchange prior to transitioning into a new FX regime. For illustrative purposes, we assume the government is able to rollover 100% of local currency amortization in 2025 at the expense of higher financial costs. On the hard currency side, for the sake of simplicity, we are assuming neither market access nor fresh IMF financing. We do assume a rollover of Paris Club and other multilateral maturities.

In such a scenario, assuming a 0.7% of GDP headline deficit next year, the net financing gap would amount to US\$8bn or just 1.0% of GDP (Table 1). Granted, if the government is able to increase the primary surplus to 2% of GDP on higher GDP growth (headline balance of +0.2% of GDP), under the same assumptions noted above, the net financing gap for next year would be zero.

Of course, new multilateral financing, loans, and/or market access would facilitate the transition further, decompressing the need for high rollover of local currency debt. In fact, the challenges arising from the increasing local currency debt stock are underlined when we stress the rollover assumption. For example, if assuming 90% of the peso debt maturities rollover, the net financing gap doubles, to US\$16bn.

Thus, while new IMF net financing, plus additional bilateral financing, could help close next year’s financing gap even under the scenario of lower than 100% local currency rollover, the exercise illustrates that both an increase in the primary surplus and recovery of market access are needed to secure the stabilization effort in the medium term.

## Australian inflation: How far from home?

● Historically, 6 months of core inflation annualizing in the target band is necessary for RBA cuts

This hurdle hasn’t looked like being cleared in 2Q, and the hike conversation remains alive near-term

Threshold is closer than it appears, enough for the RBA to stay on hold, then start easing in early 2025

The CPI distribution has normalized considerably, and services “stickiness” does not look abnormal

For much of 2024, market debate around the RBA has focused on the timing of a first rate cut. However we have expected the RBA to maintain a hiking bias while remaining on hold this year. After this week’s May monthly CPI indicator, the conversation has shifted to a potential hike in August. Though the odds of another tightening have risen, we still see the return to target as gradual, rather than markedly off track. This reflects a judgement about how lags play through the various parts of the CPI basket, the view that policy is restrictive, and that NAIRU is close (low 4s). These preconditions should help achieve the inflation target band in 2025.

Normally, a central bank should act mostly on the basis of its forecasts. In this cycle too, we expect downside on the RBA’s 2H24 forecasts (driven by recent fiscal policy changes) will allow it to look through modest upside on 2Q CPI. Forwardlooking behaviour should also allow the board to eventually cut, before the target band (2-3%) is technically achieved on an %oya basis. Even before this week’s May monthly CPI indicator, it hasn’t looked likely that %q/q core inflation would be annualizing in the target band in the June quarter. But the finer details of the CPI breakdown suggest that threshold remains plausible for the 2H24 data (quarterly prints delivered in Oct and Jan), which would set up a first cut in early 2025.

Figure 1: RBA cash rate and trimmed mean inflation
![](images/30cd0c03dbfe75674cc8ed60705164cff1c48393a4df742012fa63ff03cf89a5.jpg)

## Hurdle rates

Historically, two quarters of core inflation annualizing inside the target band has been a necessary condition for the RBA to cut rates, but not a sufficient one. For example, over the last 15 years, in every quarter in which the RBA has cut, the %6m ar trimmed mean inflation rate delivered that quarter has been below 3% (Figure 1). Over the entire inflation-targeting regime from 1996, that inflation threshold was met for around 3 in every 4 cuts. A period of sub-3% inflation has hardly been sufficient for easing however; cuts were delivered in roughly 30% of such quarters over the last 15 years.

One could argue that any inflation threshold should be even more demanding in this cycle, given the recent experience of very high inflation. But as discussed below, the policy rate is not normally starting from a position that policymakers confi dently view as restrictive, when NAIRU also appears close. There has also been much more information to judge these unobservables, than occurred under the low macro volatility regime of the 2010s. Moreover, the composition of CPI data lends itself to further disinflation, and the board’s priorities are also slightly different than in previous cycles, given the goal of not returning to the pre-COVID state of the labour market; the board now acknowledges there was slack in the labour market heading into 2020, which it didn’t in real time.

## Not there yet

With this week’s May CPI indicator in hand, we are tracking 1.0%q/q (not annualized) for 2Q headline CPI (Figure 2), and 0.9% for trimmed mean (Figure 3).

Figure 2: Headline %q/q actual, and fitted on monthlies
![](images/189753c16096b09f441f5dcab26dec8403d50b8961f4a22ee7c01852b3af08a3.jpg)

Some market participants see significant upside risk to the RBA’s standing forecast for 2Q (1%q/q) after the May data, though the mapping from monthly CPIs to quarterly is a little more complicated than a simple adding-up exercise. For one, infrequently-updated items are effectively set once for an entire quarter. So if for example a subgroup is updated only in May, that level also applies to earlier months in the calculation of quarterly CPI, information which isn’t incorporated directly when the April monthly result is released. Taking this

Trimmed mean, %q/q ar; 1Q observation marked with triangle and other nuances into account though, one can get pretty close to the quarterly rate with all months in hand (Figure 2 again, having also nowcasted the value for June).

Even after April’s data the tracking exercise for 2Q had suggested the <3%q/q ar threshold for trimmed mean was unlikely to be met in the June quarter. Looking ahead, however, it does still look achievable for 2H as government subsidies are extended and underlying increases in energy prices, rents, and insurance are less significant than last year. Some have argued that subsidies are not relevant for the RBA, as they will be trimmed out of core inflation. We disagree for a couple of reasons (for more, see here). First, the subsidies are not depressing prices in sequential terms, they are depressing prices relative to the (previously-assumed) counterfactual, where they expired in 2H. In this sense there is not an ‘extreme’ disinflation impulse from these items to be trimmed. There is also benefit via the inflation expectations channel from keeping realized inflation in high-visibility groups lower for a bit longer.

Figure 3: Trimmed mean %q/q actual, and fitted on monthlies
![](images/41a24ee5edbaaf17930d0d76c4562fdcc1ca4cabb299a233724dfd67fd0ce69a.jpg)

Third and more fundamentally, the beta from headline to trimmed mean is only weak when reshuffling items in the tails, which in this case requires the inflation change for the item in question to be small, and/or the CPI distribution to be right-skewed. Neither are true in this case: the impact of letting subsidies wane or not is worth roughly 0.5%-pt on headline inflation (a large impact), and the distribution of inflation is currently left-skewed. In the next section we look at more specific measures of how close trimmed mean is to the target band.

## Respecting boundaries

The fall in inflation in Australia has been relatively broadbased. Higher inflation outcomes have become less prevalent, which represents a move to the south west in Figure 4: the proportion of items growing above 4%q/q ar, above 3%, and above 2% has been coming down, bringing core inflation down with it. This is good news, in the sense that the RBA’s progress on inflation has not been flattered by unsustainable declines in a narrow subset of items.

Of course, one could flip this argument on its head, by noting that this means above-target inflation is a broad phenomenon. Both goods and services inflation are above normal, and this is contributing to the level of trimmed mean being above the required threshold to cut (see here and here, and Figure 4 again).

Figure 4: Trimmed mean and right tails
![](images/c28d7aa370971a6a0764237fcc829e967c10a07587dd2c8f0a3c52663b9983ff.jpg)

In our view, the bottom-up analysis suggests however that further disinflation is likely, and as discussed below, “stickiness” of services in particular is not abnormal, given the path the economy has taken. Before digging into that detail, it is worth noting that even a modest further shift to the south west in Figure 4 would see trimmed mean inflation close to the target band in %q/q annualized terms. To demonstrate, we first take an atheoretical approach, and use machine learning techniques (Support Vector Machines; SVM) to guess whether trimmed mean is annualizing in the target or not in a given quarter, based on the proportion of CPI items running above 3%q/q ar. The SVM labels ‘1’ as in the band, and ‘0’ outside the band.

Figure 5: SVM classifier (in/out of target band, given CPI distribution) 0: %q/q ar trimmed mean outside target band, 1: inside target band
![](images/eea94475999ea5673a27dc34445d680de2f98953e59ba1f7ada71c7fa2de9cdf.jpg)

As one would expect, both a very high, and very low share of items running above 3%q/q ar lead the SVM to guess outside the band (Figure 5), while intermediate values lead to a guess that core inflation is annualizing inside the band. This fitted classification scheme, based only on the proportion of items above 3%q/q ar is correct 71% of the time, compared to a naïve rule which guesses outside the band (the most common outcome) every time, which is successful 58% of the time. The SVM guessed inside the band in 1Q24. That is, even 1Q’s CPI data were more historically consistent with trimmed mean annualizing in the target band, than outside, which shows that the required thresholds are not so distant.

## You must trust or life becomes impossible

The main practical justification for the RBA hiking, or waiting longer than usual to cut is risk management, after a period of very high inflation. However, while it is true that inflation, and inflation volatility have been much higher in recent years, the other perspective is that this has delivered much greater variation to test the determinants of inflation, and the impact of interest rates on the economy, than in the pre-pandemic years.

The data suggest policy is restrictive, the economy is operating below potential, and will continue to do so, even with some pick-up near-term. Further, NAIRU appears close (in the low 4s). With wage-determination being very slow-moving, shocks appear to transmit from prices in product markets, into wages in the labour market, rather than vice versa, and inflation expectations are anchored. All these factors suggest that inflation should continue converging to target, particularly if there are still lagged effects of earlier tightening feeding through.

Figure 6: 1m-ahead R^2 in models of CPI groups R^2 in 1m-ahead regression of monthly CPI, across groups
![](images/7c8a0d1fa69022f82e89c5553ba866365212d868aff9ed91b3e6465d493dc42a.jpg)

There is also access to more high frequency tracking data for inflation than there used to be in previous cycles (monthly CPI was introduced in 2022, with a back series to 2017). And using this data, the services components expected to pose most risk of a further delay in return to target are also somewhat easier to forecast in the short-run (Figure 6). These include health, education, recreation/culture, and finance/ insurance. These groups have heavy seasonal factors, less frequent price resets, as well as significant correlation to business surveys, other price inputs, and the lags of headline inflation itself. Using a standardized set of regression models with such inputs, the average R^2 in forecasting month-ahead %m/m inflation in these groups is 0.85, compared to an average of 0.67 across all groups.

Figure 7: Services inflation, fitted on goods and lagged headline
![](images/5c6063c5d5d493d05e426b6a2508206f2c6aab7451ff9715b6ac40bad5775148.jpg)
Source: ABS, J.P. Morgan

Lastly, we note that while services inflation is elevated and has come down relatively less than goods, not all inflation is created equal. Services inflation is normally higher, and is slower to respond to cyclical conditions than is goods inflation. The lagging nature of services inflation can in fact give the central bank greater confidence, if the pre-conditions for a further decline have been set in motion.

The most important preconditions are that GDP and headline inflation have already slowed a lot, both of which should be feeding through to CPI-sensitive rental, insurance, and household service inflation in particular (not to mention tobacco, which is a good, but is counted in non-tradable inflation typically associated with services). The lag structure, or broader relationship from headline inflation back to services inflation also doesn’t appear significantly different from history. For example, a simple regression of services inflation on goods inflation, and (4Q-ago) lags of headline inflation has a residual near zero (Figure 7). So, services categories are not showing any more stickiness than is normal, given their lagging properties. Taking into account the bottom-up drivers and the composition of CPI as discussed above, in our view core inflation remains on track to move much closer to the target band in %q/q ar terms over 2H24.

## China: No fireworks from the 3rd Plenum

The 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum is likely to reiterate the priorities laid out in the 2017 Party Congress

It is unlikely to change the current framework, including the direction of policies & reform priorities

Key areas to look out for include a balanced strategy to promote productivity, rural-land and fiscal reforms

The 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum of the 20<sup>th</sup> Party Congress will be held in July. The CPC Party Congress is held every five years, and there are seven Plenary Sessions in between two Party Congresses. Each Plenary Session usually has a pre-determined focus, with the 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum usually focusing on economic issues and hence getting more attention from investors.

## 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum has a history of policy innovation ...

The 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum has, in the past, seen critical moments of directional change in policy and reforms, starting with the 1978 session that shifted focus to economic growth and liberalization and its influence lasted right up to the 2017 Party Congress. In the decades that followed, several such sessions, usually in even-number Party Congresses (e.g. 14<sup>th</sup>, 16<sup>th</sup> and 18<sup>th</sup> Party Congress), have been the forum for introducing major conceptual innovation in policy framework, while those in odd-number Party Congresses usually focus on reforms in specific areas (e.g. price and wage reform, rural area reform, see Table 1). This raises speculation whether the July meeting (another even-number) will deliver conceptual innovation in the economic framework.

Table 1: List of 3rd Plenum of CPC Party Congress
<table><tr><td>Party Congress Date</td><td></td><td>Main outcome</td></tr><tr><td>11th</td><td>Dec 18-22, 1978</td><td>Shift the focus of Party work to socialist modernization and economic work. A directional change.</td></tr><tr><td>12th</td><td>Oct 20, 1984</td><td>Reform from rural to urban areas, break-through in key concepts like commodity economy and law of value</td></tr><tr><td>13th</td><td>Sep 26-30, 1988</td><td>Price and wage reform; strengthen and improve the ideological and political work in enterprises</td></tr><tr><td>14th</td><td>Nov 11-14, 1993</td><td>Lay out the framework of socialist market economy system.</td></tr><tr><td>15th</td><td>Oct 12-14, 1998</td><td>New socialist rural area developments</td></tr><tr><td>16th</td><td>Oct 11-14, 2003</td><td>Improve socialist market economy system concept, scientific development. Shareholding is a main form of public ownership, establish modern property rights system. Non-SOE entities can enter any field unless prohibited.</td></tr><tr><td>17th</td><td>Oct 9-12, 2008</td><td>Rural area reform, especially transferability of rural land contrracting and management rights</td></tr><tr><td>18th</td><td>Nov 9-12, 2013</td><td>Comprehensive elaboration on main reform areas, market should play a decisive role in resource allocation</td></tr><tr><td>19th</td><td>Feb 26-28, 2018</td><td>Institution reforms for the Party and the government</td></tr></table>

Sources: Party documents

## … but likely no big surprises this time

However, we do not expect the upcoming 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum in July will deliver major surprises or conceptual developments in economic policy framework. In the past 3-4 decades, when the 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum has delivered policy innovation, it has always coincided with the change in top leadership. This is not the case this time, with President Xi extending his third term at the 20<sup>th</sup> Party Congress.

Hence, most likely the 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum in July will provide a comprehensive summary of key reform areas that have been proposed in recent years, rather than directional or conceptual changes in policy framework. In particular, we expect the plenum to reiterate the blueprint as laid out at the 19<sup>th</sup> Party Congress (2017) and reinforced at the 20<sup>th</sup> Party Congress (2022). Table 2 summarizes main outcomes from the Party Congresses since 1977.

Table 2: List of CPC Party Congress
<table><tr><td>Party Congress Date</td><td></td><td>Main outcome</td></tr><tr><td>11th</td><td>Aug 12-18, 1977</td><td>End of Cultural Revolution; Continue to emphasize class struggle; Built China into a modernized socialist country by end of this century</td></tr><tr><td>12th</td><td>Sep 1-11, 1982</td><td>First propose &quot;building socialism with Chinese characteristics&quot;</td></tr><tr><td>13th</td><td>Oct 25-Nov 1, 1987</td><td>China&#x27;s socialism is still at primary stage; propose &quot;one center (economic development), two pillars (four basic principles + reform &amp; openness)&quot;</td></tr><tr><td>14th</td><td>Oct 12-18, 1992</td><td>Deng Xiaoping Theory as guidance, accelerate reform, opening up and modemnization; GDP growth target lifted to 8-9%</td></tr><tr><td>15th</td><td>Sep 12-18, 1997</td><td>Deng Xiaoping Theory as guidance; Public ownership as the main pillar, co- developments of multiple ownerships</td></tr><tr><td>16th</td><td>Nov 8-14, 2002</td><td>Deng theory + Three representatives; build a moderately prosperous society</td></tr><tr><td>17th</td><td>Oct 15-21, 2007</td><td>Deng&#x27;s theory + Three representatives + scientific development; build a moderately propsperous society by 2020; revision of Party Constitution</td></tr><tr><td>18th</td><td>Nov 8-14, 2012</td><td>Deng&#x27;s theory + Three representatives + scientific development; build a moderately propsperous society by 2020</td></tr><tr><td>19th</td><td>Oct 18-24, 2017</td><td>Xi&#x27;s thoughts on socialism with Chinese Characteristics under a new era; China dream of great rejuvenation of the Chinese nation; change in main contradictiion between people&#x27;s growting needs for a better life and the unbalanced and inadequate development.</td></tr><tr><td>20th</td><td>Oct 16-22, 2022</td><td>Xi&#x27;s thoughts on socialism with Chinese Characteristics under a new era; Build a moder socialiist country; the Party&#x27;s second Centenary goal</td></tr></table>

Sources: Party documents

The 19<sup>th</sup> Party Congress delivered the most important document under Xi’s leadership, which in our view defined China’s watershed moment in that it ushered a decisive transition from a nearly four-decade long governance and economic framework established by Deng Xiaoping (China’s 2.0) to a new development strategy (China’s 3.0). The 19<sup>th</sup> Party Congress redefined the “principal contradiction” facing Chinese society as the tension “between unbalanced and inadequate development and people’s ever-growing needs for a better life”, replacing the previous assessment of the tension “between the ever-growing material and cultural needs of the people and backward social productivity” (6th Plenary Session of the 11<sup>th</sup> Party Congress in 1981, replacing the class struggle between the proletariat and the bourgeoisie as identified by Mao Zedong’s China 1.0). New development or highquality growth became the new framework to replace the economic system in the past 40 years that focused on generating growth and employment. Instead, the focus is now on bal-

anced objectives with slower growth, green transition, innovation-driven growth, tightened regulation, limited leverage, security, etc. The new policy framework was further strengthened at the 20<sup>th</sup> Party Congress in 2022.

## Actions matter more than talk

Another reason for relatively low expectations for the 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum in July is that, for investors, policy actions and policy implementation matter more than policy talk. A restatement of pro-market or pro-reform policies is welcomed, but the gap between talk and actions has been a source of disappointment.

For instance, some policy guidance has stayed on paper and lacked effective implementation. Such examples include a series of Party documents since 2H23 to ensure equal treatment between SOEs and non-SOEs and restore confidence among private entrepreneurs, and the recent emphasis on boosting domestic demand. A lack of concrete policy actions has led to disappointment and weakened market confidence.

In some cases, policy guidance may point in the right direction, in our view, but poor policy implementation undermines the initial policy objective. For instance, the policymakers made the right decision to address excesses in the housing market in late 2020, but overly tight regulation and delayed policy adjustment amplified the negative impact on the economy and markets. Another example is the de-carbonization effort: in 2021 there was campaign-style implementation (e.g. shutting down high energy-intensity factories) which led to an energy shortage in 3Q21, and the problem was fixed only after a dramatic policy adjustment in 2022.

## What reforms are important at this stage?

While we have relatively low expectations for the 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum in July, it is still an important policy event. At a high level, it is important to watch whether the policy framework will maintain a balance among multiple objectives as in recent years (status quo), or shift the focus to growth (e.g. reiteration “to take economic development as the central task”), or shift to ideology or security-based policy consideration. “Status quo” is our baseline scenario.

Concretely, the list of structural reform areas will provide an agenda for priority economic work in the next 3-5 years. Following the list of meetings on reforms after the 20<sup>th</sup> Party Congress (Table 3), key reform areas may include: new-quality productive forces (i.e. promote innovation and productivity increase); green transformation; fiscal reform; SOE reform; digital economy and data as a new input factor for production; rural area reform; a unified national market; high-level opening up; financial reforms that include capital market developments and risk prevention, etc.

Promote innovations via opening-up</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Feb 19, 2024 Land management system; Green transformation of the Chinese economy; Promote innovations</td></tr><tr><td>Nov 7, 2023</td><td>Promote the construction of a beautiful China; Improve state-owned capital operation budget system; Improve the regulatory mechanism for natural monopolies; Experts participation in public decision- making process; Zoning control of the ecological environment</td></tr><tr><td>Jul 11, 2023</td><td>Higher-level opening-up to promote new development model; Support China&#x27;s modernization with high-quality population development</td></tr><tr><td>Central Financial Commission</td><td></td><td>Mar 13, 2024 Study Xi&#x27;s speech at Two Sessions, implement government work report;
