Five</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>key areas including innovations, green, inclusive, pension and digital finance; Strengthen financial regulation and prevent financial risks; Overarching Party leadership on financial work</td></tr></table>

Source: Party documents.

In our view, 3 reform areas are most important at this stage. Any significant progress in 1 of the 3 areas, followed by effective implementation, will make us more positive about China’s medium- to long-term growth outlook (link).

First is a more balanced strategy to promote innovation and productivity gains. The current approach has narrowly emphasized advanced manufacturing sectors, especially after the success in “new three-wonder sectors” (new energy, lithium battery and new energy vehicles). The government is doubling down on industry policies in manufacturing, e.g. semiconductor, low-altitude economy, commercial airplane, new material, AI, etc. However, productivity increase also comes from upgrade of services and conventional manufacturing (as stated in this year’s government work report and President Xi’s recent speech).

China’s past high-growth era was been associated with faster expansion in service sectors (Figure 1). However, services (e.g. education, internet, healthcare, real estate, financial services) have met with policy challenges in recent years, in contrast to support for advanced manufacturing. This has raised unemployment among highly-skilled workers (including college graduates), weakened consumption among uppermiddle income families and dragged on economic growth. Therefore, promoting productivity increase should be based on equal treatment between manufacturing and service upgrade, and equal treatment between SOEs and non-SOEs (including foreign companies).

Figure 1: Secondary vs. tertiary sectors in China
![](images/d24b47dee9b9177fd92379d82be69912507d5c27c728c288fcf9d43315258559.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

Second is rural land reform, specifically allowing rural homes to be tradeable, freeing funds to finance public housing and the social safety network in the new urbanization process. The large gap between China’s urbanization ratio and hukou ratio (residence vs citizenship based, at 66% and 48% respectively, in 2023), 18% of total population (or 250 million people), represents a major opportunity for above-average growth going forward (Figure 2).

In the housing market, replacement home demand from the beneficiary households in hukou reform (largely low and middle income families) provides lots of room for the development of the public and rental housing market. This could partially offset the structural decline in commodity housing. In the near term, the funding for public / rental housing can come from the central bank (e.g. re-lending facility introduced in May) or special government bonds. But sustainable longer-term funding will come from the value increase in rural land reform, with the revenue to be split between beneficiary households and local governments.

Figure 2: China's urbanization ratios
![](images/ffa47b443dd51e7431f52f98adf8faeae8220efd8e19f21005d0fd4a13162607.jpg)

For instance, the “land ticket” experiment in Chongqing about a decade ago allowed farmers to convert allocated rural home land into a certificate (land ticket) that was transferable or used as collateral. Chongqing government can change the usage of rural land and the value increase was used to build large-scale public housing to support the urbanization process. The experiment was successful, but expanding it nationwide will involve the difficulty of land resources reallocation across regions. Most migrant workers work in provinces different from their hometown, so there is a geographic mismatch between rural land supply from migrant workers and demand for public/rental housing. Also, the political concern that farmland is the last resort for migrant workers in business downturns (so as to avoid China’s version of slum areas in cities) has also constrained progress in rural land reform.

Third is fiscal reform. Fiscal reform has become more urgent lately, due to rising concerns about the fiscal condition of local governments and the LGFV debt problem. Fiscal reform should include three elements (Figures 3 and 4). First is a new structure between central and local governments to address the misalignment in revenues and expenditures. Second is reform of fiscal revenues, considering what new sources of fiscal revenue could be introduced. A recent IMF Paper (link) recommended increasing the effectiveness of labor income taxation, improving capital income taxation, improving property and wealth taxation and leveraging international corporate income tax reform. Third, but most importantly, is stricter discipline of fiscal expenditure (including preventing new increases in local government hidden debt). This may involve changing functions for local governments from investmentoriented to service-oriented, and the challenge in doing so should not be underestimated.

Figure 3: China's fiscal revenue vs. expenditure
![](images/1d94832d07a4092e54cc67a56c1613f3acb63a1135e3b1c4d58f8c407b151d51.jpg)

Figure 4: Central government's fiscal role
![](images/13ed7fd48ee6798ff6517865923508507f530adf068467679f96c4286834dd68.jpg)

BoK’s new communication tool

## Korea: A Festina Lente strategy for adjusting stance

The BoK’s signals for a rate cut remain cautious to balance timing risks

We believe the risks of a too-early shift outweigh those of a too-late shift in the near term

BoK dot-plot and MPC minutes do not suggest an immediate policy shift in 3Q

• We expect a dissent vote for a rate cut in 3Q, with an actual cut in 4Q24

Over the past few weeks, expectations for a rate cut by the Bank of Korea in 3Q24 have increased in financial markets. This reflects the initiation of rate cuts by some developed market central banks (such as the European Central Bank) and views that a Federal Reserve policy shift may not be far off. Additionally, comments from some government officials suggesting an approaching rate cut have likely influenced these expectations.

However, the BoK's policy signals regarding a rate cut remain cautious. The BoK itself has described this as a 'Festina Lente' approach, aiming to balance the risks of acting too late and too early. According to the BoK, the risks of a too-early shift include delayed convergence of inflation to the target, increased FX rate volatility, and a re-acceleration of household leverage growth. Conversely, the risks of a too-late shift include a widening gap between robust exports and softer domestic demand, as well as financial market instability likely due to delinquencies in real estate market-related leverages.

We believe that the risks associated with a too-early shift will remain greater than those of a too-late shift over the next 2-3 months. Our review of the MPC minutes and the BoK's improved communication tools, such as the dot plot, indicates that a significant change in the BoK's economic and policy assessment from the May meeting would be required for a rate cut to occur during 3Q24. Given this context, we maintain our view that signals for a rate cut will intensify in 3Q24, with an actual rate cut likely to occur in 4Q24.

## Contained near-term downside risk

In the May meeting, the BoK judged that maintaining the current tight stance was appropriate, considering the improvement in GDP growth and the increased upside risks to inflation. Nevertheless, if the BoK were to initiate a rate cut cycle in 3Q24, it would require a significant increase in downside risks to growth and inflation compared to the BoK's assessment in May. In our view, such downside risks remain limited

for the time being.

2Q24 growth will likely decline from the strong 5.3% q/q, saar growth in 1Q24. However, on average for 1H24, growth is expected to be around 3% oya, overshooting the potential rate (Figure 1). While domestic demand growth is mixed and relatively soft compared to net exports, the 1Q GDP data confirmed that it is not weak enough to necessitate an urgent policy rate adjustment. Moreover, the July MPC meeting will be held before the 2Q24 growth rate is confirmed, and there will not be much new and significant information on domestic demand compared to the May meeting. Therefore, it will be difficult for MPC members to significantly alter their macroeconomic outlook in July from that in the May meeting.

Figure 1: Real GDP growth
![](images/7743d36c82e895eaf13b80703915a6c7c395dc21a1d770a976d2a49b515a98f2.jpg)

In recent months CPI inflation has shown signs of slowing towards the target, which supports the rationale for easing the degree of policy tightness. However, headline inflation is only gradually stabilizing and is expected to be around the upper 2%oya range by August (Figure 2), according to our projections. This outlook aligns with the BoK’s May projection. And the MPC minutes saw one member (in our view, likely the Senior Deputy Governor) emphasize the necessity of maintaining a sufficiently tight stance until the stability of inflation expectations can be assured.

Figure 2: Inflation forecast
![](images/3aa35a70a0bc3f528dd74999ec2af71afb7a9010d57fd8a870a2c24e4c46fada.jpg)

Since 4Q22, the BoK has begun to supplement its verbal policy guidance with more specific quantitative indicators, disclosing individual members' views on the likelihood of policy rate changes within the next three months. Although a sufficiently long time series of cases has not yet been accumulated, the track record so far suggests that the BoK-style dot plot provides useful information on the path of rates. In the most recent dot plot presented at the May MPC meeting, only one out of six members was open to the possibility of a rate cut within the next three months. Therefore, if the current data trajectory remains aligned with the BoK's forecast path, there are not enough signals to suggest a rate cut in 3Q24, making it more reasonable to expect the first rate cut to occur no earlier than 4Q24.

Figure 3 compares the simple average of the possible policy rate changes within the next three months as indicated by individual members in the BoK-style dot plot (BoK's signal), the forecasted change in the 3-month CD rate three months ahead as determined by the forward rate agreement market (market expectation), and the actual policy rate change three months later (actual actions).

Figure 3: BoK's signal, market expectation, and actual actions bps, policy rate changes in three months
![](images/50fd5f1b92b1bf48318b85d0e45bbcc9bf9fe39af2f9cf0ffde82e9e7c5092ac.jpg)

Examining the cases over time, the BoK-style dot plot was first introduced during the final phase of the rate hike cycle and accurately conveyed the magnitude of the rate increases to some degree. In October 2022, a majority of members indicated a 3.5% rate, while some members expected a lower rate. We interpreted this as four members expecting 3.5% and two members (who were dovish dissenters in October 2022) expecting 3.25%. Indeed, the rate was raised to 3.5% three months later in January 2023. Additionally, in November 2022, the average of the members' opinions was 3.54%, signaling a 25bp increase, which was realized with a 25bp hike in January of the following year.

The hawkish signals from the BoK-style dot plot from February to November 2023 did not materialize. During that period, most members indicated the possibility of an additional 25bp hike, which influenced market expectations to some extent. However, such hawkish signals were merely mentions of the possibility of a hike. Starting in 2024, these hawkish biases have disappeared, and signals regarding the possibility of a rate cut within the year have begun. Nevertheless, it is reasonable to conclude that there is not yet a strong intention to implement rate changes in the near term based on this dot plot.

## What the members have said

As previously mentioned, the overall opinion of the MPC and the BoK's institutional view do not strongly signal a rate cut in the very near term. However, individual members' assessments of the costs and benefits of maintaining the tight policy stance vary, according to the MPC minutes. In our interpretation of the anonymous minutes (Figure 4), there is one dovish member who may dissent for a rate cut in the near future (presumed to be Shin), one neutral-dovish member who is more concerned about the imbalance between domestic demand and exports but may not immediately cast a dissent vote (presumed to be Hwang), one neutral member who prefers to wait and see further data inflows (presumed to be Lee), and three neutral-hawkish members who emphasize the upside risks to inflation, caution against FX volatility, or the need for de-leveraging efforts (presumed to be the Senior Deputy Governor, Chang, and Kim).

## Figure 4: MPC hawk-dove chart

Chang, Yongsung

Senior deputy governor

Kim, Jong Hwa

Lee, Soohyung
Hwang, Kunil
<table><tr><td>Shin, Sung Hwan</td></tr><tr><td>Neutral</td></tr><tr><td>Hawk Dove Source: J.P. Morgan&#x27;s reading on MPC minutes</td></tr></table>

The most dovish member indicated that conditions for a rate cut are gradually being met from an inflation perspective, and while there are some concerns over the FX rate, a sharp depreciation of the KRW is unlikely. Our assessment suggests this member's dovishness increased from April to May, indicating a possibility of a dissent vote in 3Q24. However, other members remain cautious about the upside risks to inflation, the risk of policy easing re-accelerating household leverage growth, and the impact of a preemptive rate cut on FX volatility. To sum up, the MPC's stance remains predominantly hawkish, and a few more months may be needed before implementing a rate cut.

## United States

• May core PCE at 0.08% should help build some confidence for continued disinflation

• Real consumer spending looks to be downshifting in 1H

• Housing market revival has lost momentum

• We look for 200K payroll gains in June, steady 4% unemployment rate

This week attention was squarely focused on the looming presidential election. For the economy-minded, the outcome of that election will shape the macro backdrop next year and beyond. We provide a brief sketch of the relevant policy issues in an accompanying note. Meanwhile the data flow marches on, and this week’s figures point to only modest growth in current economic activity.

Perhaps more important for markets and Fed-watchers was the very soft reading on the May core PCE price index, the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge. Core PCE prices advanced only 0.08% last month, taking the year-ago reading down to 2.6%. The soft sequential increase—the lowest in over three years—suggests the disinflationary trends that were interrupted in 1Q are getting back on track. We still think this trend will continue, giving the Fed enough comfort to begin lowering the funds rate starting in 4Q.

Economy-wide spending growth appears to have moderated recently. Real consumer spending increased a respectable 0.3% last month, but this came after a modest decline in April and it looks like consumption growth this quarter is running a little shy of a 2% annual rate. This slowing comes after we also learned this week that consumption growth only expanded at a downward-revised 1.5% annual pace in 1Q.

Indicators on the interest-sensitive sectors also came in light this week. Real business investment spending on equipment in 2Q will likely benefit from a bounceback in civilian aircraft outlays (a very volatile category), as well as growth in vehicle sales and imported capital goods. But this week’s data for domestic manufacturers’ May orders and shipments for nondefense capital goods excluding aircraft dipped, and have been moving sideways over much of the past year.

After showing some hints of recovery earlier in the year, most housing figures have turned south again more recently. That trend continued this week, as both new and pending home sales fell materially in May. Inventories of unsold new homes have been creeping higher recently, and we think this will weigh on home building in the second half, something we discuss more in this week’s Focus.

The labor market news this week was mixed. Initial jobless claims eased off a little, although continuing claims grinded up to the highest since late 2021. The labor market will remain front and center in next week’s holiday-shortened calendar. We get the May JOLTS report on Tuesday and the June employment report on Friday. For the latter we expect nonfarm payrolls to rise 200k and the unemployment rate to be unchanged at 4.0%.

## Cooler inflation, softer consumption growth

Core PCE price growth for May, at 0.083% to three decimals, was the softest monthly reading since November 2020 (Figure 1). Services excluding housing was also only up 0.1%m/ m (3.4%oya), and headline inflation was flat (2.6%oya). These data are good news for the Fed, though FOMC members will want to see more low readings to gain confidence that inflation is moving back to 2%. Over the last three months core inflation rose 2.7% saar, down from a recent peak of 4.5% in March (Figure 2). While consistent with the story that the firm inflation we saw in 1Q was a bump amidst a gradual moderation, the May month-on-month change probably overstates how quickly core price growth will drop. Even so, we think that the FOMC will take enough comfort from this trend to begin lowering the funds rate starting in 4Q.

Figure 1: PCE price aggregates
![](images/3f95e486b386a33ee0e51a25c4d01833234ba9d9df9782f0d910baee3f5bfd3b.jpg)

Figure 2: Core PCE inflation run rates
![](images/ec1749a3f5224e0f8bd9701446248641d5179dec68887abe938a9e3c18e03898.jpg)

Real consumer spending rose 0.3%m/m (0.26%) in May, which brought the 2Q run-rate to 1.4%q/q, saar, and we now see a risk that the full 2Q growth could come in a bit below 2%. Growth for the first quarter had originally been reported at 2.5%, then 2.0% after Thursday’s final GDP report that had been revised down to 1.5%. 1H24 consumption growth around 1.5% is a notable downshift from 2H23, when growth ran a touch over 3%. That said, relatively healthy labor markets and wage gains should still provide some support for consumers. The May personal income and outlays data offered some evidence for this, with nominal personal income increasing 0.5%m/m and compensation up 0.6%m/m. With overall prices flat during the month real DPI increased a robust 0.5%m/m, the strongest gain seen within the past year. For the quarter, however, real DPI is increasing at a milder 1.4% annualized pace, similar to the 1.3% gain in 1Q.

## Some weakness in core capital goods

The details of the durable goods report were somewhat soft in May. Core capital goods shipments (nondefense ex aircraft) slid 0.5%m/m and are down at a 1.9%q/q, saar pace so far in 2Q, while core capital goods orders fell 0.6%m/m. On a 12- month basis both core capital goods shipments and orders are roughly unchanged (Figure 3). Moreover, regional Fed surveys of capex intentions point to little if any growth in core shipments in coming months, although the relationship between these surveys and the shipments data is loose. Extransportation shipments are tracking a 0.7%q/q, saar increase in 2Q, while total shipments are up at a 6.8% pace, reflecting 48%q/q, saar growth in volatile civilian aircraft shipments. Thus we still think overall equipment spending will be a boost for 2Q output growth despite the soft details in this week’s May report. This is also consistent with the strength in capex we have been tracking globally.

Durable goods orders and shipments
![](images/b484ea517ff7e77ae5d84913633146e3db282751c655e1f422cdfaf01973cbeb.jpg)

## The housing revival, no more

The prospects of high-for-longer interest rates are again weighing on the housing recovery. After some glimmers of hope for the sector earlier in the year, recent readings from the housing market have been weaker. This week both new and pending home sales dropped in May. New home sales were down 11.3%m/m to a 619k annualized pace, but the blow was cushioned by material upward revisions to the prior three months. The May reading is the lowest since November 2022, but with the upward revisions it would be too early to conclude that new home sales are breaking lower for good. Similarly, the index of pending home sales, which lead exist ing home sales by one to two months, declined 2.1%m/m (-6.6%oya) to a new all-time low (ex-COVID) in May, signaling ongoing weakness in the existing home market. While a new low, the most recent reading is a just a bit below other recent observations. We think these recent readings imply that residential investment will fall 4-5% during 2H. For more on the single-family side of this, see this week’s Focus.

## Calmer claims ahead of solid jobs report

Ahead of a week full of labor market data, initial jobless claims fell for the second consecutive week after heading higher for a number of weeks prior, which may have been partially related to noise around the earlier holidays. Initial claims pulled back to 233k in the week ending June 22, from 239k (previously 238k) in the prior week (Figure 4). On the other hand, continuing claims rose to 1.839m in the week ending June 15, from 1.821m. This is a new post-COVID high and is something to keep an eye on, though the latest reading isn’t that far above values in 4Q23.

Figure 4: Jobless claims
![](images/cd7ce7f3d4680956936ff0153bed423b574ab43807fabc42a9ea4ed5e42cd609.jpg)

The highlight of the holiday-shortened week next week wil be the June jobs report. While we continue to look for the labor market to gradually cool over the rest of this year, we look for a firm report next Friday with nonfarm payrolls gaining 200k and the unemployment rate holding steady at 4.0%. Pandemic-distorted services, such as education and health care, as well as government, are likely to again account for the bulk of the job growth. Wage growth should also remain fairly firm, with average hourly earnings expected to rise 0.3% on the month. But this should put year-ago growth in earnings below 4% for the first time since mid-2021, another encouraging sign for the Fed. Earlier next week the May JOLTS report is expected to show job openings slipped below 8 million for the first time since early 2021—a further easing of labor demand and an indication that the labor market is continuing to gradually move into better balance.

<table><tr><td colspan="4">Mon S&amp;P Global manufacturing PMI (US) Index, sa</td></tr><tr><td>Jul 1 9:45am</td><td colspan="3"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td>Flash</td><td>Final</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Jun 23</td><td>May 24</td><td>Jun 24</td><td>Jun 24</td></tr><tr><td>Composite</td><td>46.3</td><td>51.3</td><td>50.9</td><td>51.5</td></tr><tr><td>Output</td><td>46.9</td><td>53.0</td><td>52.4</td><td></td></tr></table>

We look for the final manufacturing PMI for June to remain near the 51.7 reading that was given in the earlier flash report. The PMI has been slowly increasing over the last year, an improvement that is slightly better than what we’ve seen from the ISM manufacturing or regional Fed surveys (for more detail, see the ISM manufacturing forecast). Nonetheless, the composite and output measures remain near the lower end of the range typically observed in economic expansions.

While the composite and output indexes in the report are on the lower end of the range seen in expansions, the employment measure has recently been looking stronger. This index does not provide much information for forecasting typical month-to-month variation in manufacturing payrolls, but it had sent a worrying signal late last year when it fell to the lowest level observed during periods of economic growth. Since then it has risen for six consecutive months and is now back to more reassuring levels.

The ISM manufacturing index was probably largely stable in June vs. the May value of 48.7. The average of the ISMweighted composites across four regional Fed surveys (NY, Philly, Richmond, KC) declined to 47.7 this month from 48.4, pointing to a decline in the ISM, but the S&P PMI improved in its flash release, to 51.7 from 51.3. Both the ISM and the PMI surveys have improved slightly over the last year, while the average of the regional Fed surveys has moved slightly sideways. On balance the message from these various surveys is that manufacturing remains soft into the middle part of the year.

Within the survey components the new orders index was particularly weak in May, dropping to 45.4, the lowest level in a year. In contrast, the new business index in the manufacturing

PMI rose in both May and June, so new orders may recover a bit this month.

We think that construction for May will have declined 0.3%m/m as residential construction is running into a weak patch while private nonresidential and public spending have both recently leveled off after rapidly increasing over the last couple years.

Mapping the monthly data on housing starts and units under construction into monthly changes in nominal residential spending is not straightforward, but to give a sense of the trends in this area, total starts are down 41% on a 3m/3m annualized basis and units under construction are down 12%. Multifamily units under construction are still quite elevated but starts have declined sharply, so construction activity should soften for a while in this segment, and likewise singlefamily starts have recently declined as builders deal with rising inventories. Meanwhile, the lack of existing home sales should continue to dampen home improvement spending.

Job openings declined by 754,000 over the last two prints, bringing the level to its lowest since February 2021. We think that after two large monthly drops, job openings will continue to move down in May, albeit at a slower pace, bringing the level to 7.95 million openings. Several survey measures about labor market expectations including the Conference Board’s labor market differential and NFIB’s related measures on hiring plans and difficulty hiring have firmed up a bit in May. If realized, our forecast for job openings for May will bring the vacancy to unemployment ratio to 1.2, similar to the level of tightness we observed just prior to the pandemic.

If the forecast is realized, then light vehicle sales would be little changed from a year ago but up a solid 14%q/q annualized in 2Q.

The ADP employment report has been a decent leading indicator of private nonfarm payrolls as reported by the BLS in recent months, although we view it as an alternative measure of job changes as opposed to a straightforward predictor of the BLS figure. When comparing the first print of the ADP release against the first print of the BLS release the average absolute error in the last six months is 74k, and when comparing the first ADP print to the revised BLS print the error is 56k. Over the same period the BLS data show a cumulative increase in payrolls of 1,210k while the first print of ADP shows 939k, so on average the first prints have had a mild downward bias over that period.

Pavroll survey week</td><td>230</td><td>236</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="8"></td></tr></table>

Initial jobless claims have declined in each of the last two weeks after steadily rising since late April and we expect a further small decline to 230k in the week ending June 29 from 233k in the prior week. Some of the more recent gains might be partly related to seasonal adjustment issues: for example claims rose temporarily around a similar time last year, and there could be some seasonal volatility around the late-May Memorial Day holiday. In any case, it remains important from the perspective of the labor market to see if claims will pull back in coming weeks or punch higher again.

Continuing claims hit a new post-COVID high in the week ending June 15, although they are not that far above levels recorded late last year. Continuing claims may increase in coming weeks given the recent rise in initial claims.

We expect that the nominal trade balance widened to -\$77.4bn in May from -\$74.6bn in April. The advance data on goods trade was already released and it showed exports declining 2.7%m/m and imports falling 0.7%m/m, which widened the goods deficit by \$2.7bn. For services, exports and imports have been quite stable the last couple months, a break from generally rising levels that will likely resume soon. The services balance should be fairly stable. After shrinking during and after COVID it rebounded sharply through early 2023 and has since been more stable near the pre-COVID level.

<table><tr><td rowspan="2">Wed Jul 3</td><td colspan="5">S&amp;P Global services PMI (US)</td></tr><tr><td>Index, sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td>Final</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="4">9:45am</td><td></td><td>Jun 23</td><td>May 24</td><td>Flash Jun 24</td><td>Jun 24</td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>54.4</td><td>54.8</td><td>55.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td>55.0</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>52.2</td><td>49.4</td><td>51.4</td><td></td></tr></table>

The services PMI business activity index increased to 55.1 in the June flash report from 54.8 in May, which is just slightly above where it was in May 2023 and otherwise the best reading since the spring of 2022. We expect it to remain largely unchanged in the final report. Some of the other measures in the report don’t look quite as strong as business activity, for example the new business and employment indexes aren’t as high relative to their own recent history and are on the low end of the pre-COVID range, so the headline general business activity may be due to retreat a bit in future reports.

The input and output price measures in the services PMI have both generally been trending lower, with the output price index now back to broadly normal levels relative to the prepandemic period and output prices in the upper part of that

range. While a small further decline in input price measures would likely be welcome, neither price measure is particularly worrisome from an inflation standpoint.

<table><tr><td>Wed Jul 3 Sa 10:00am</td><td>ISM services survey</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Headline composite</td><td>51.4</td><td>49.4</td><td>Jun 53.8 53.0</td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>57.4</td><td>50.9 61.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>54.4</td><td>52.2</td><td>54.1</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td>48.5</td><td>45.9</td><td>47.1</td></tr><tr><td>Prices</td><td>53.4</td><td>59.2</td><td>58.1</td></tr></table>

The ISM services composite likely declined a small amount in June after a sizable gain in May, when the index jumped to 53.8 from 49.4 in April. Both the business activity and supplier deliveries indexes were up sharply last month and may retrace some of their gain, although there is room for improvement in the new orders and employment indexes, which are both on the low side of their historical range during expansions. These are contributing to the overall ISM composite maintaining a level that is low relative to the pre-pandemic period even as GDP growth and payroll gains have both been solid.

The results from other services surveys released during the month have generally improved relative to May. General business activity in the services PMI edged up slightly in June, and the gap between the May level of the ISM business activity index and the June PMI metric is not unusually large (ISM is usually higher). Looking at the regional Fed surveys, general activity indexes rose in the Dallas and Philadelphia surveys, but weakened in the Richmond survey.

The employment index has been notably weak in this survey recently. Despite moving up a bit in May, to 47.1, the index would normally signal an economy tipping into recession at these levels. While some surveys have been consistently weaker post-COVID, the decline in the employment index only began late last year. The message from payrolls has certainly been more optimistic, but it will be useful to watch if this index starts pushes lower again.

We look for nonfarm payroll gains of 200k in the June employment report and for the unemployment rate to hold steady at 4.0%. Payrolls have risen by an average of 249k/ month over the last three months and by 230k/month over the last year. For some time we have expected a gradual slowing in payrolls, given that (a) job growth averaged under 200k/ month for every year between 2016 and 2019, (b) the unemployment rate has been slowly increasing, and (c) participation excluding the oldest workers is already likely at or near a cycle high.

One contributor to recent robust job growth is probably immigration inflows, which is likely also a reason that employment gains in the establishment survey have been notably greater than in the household survey, where the payroll-adjusted concept of employment has only increased by 18k/month in the last year. In addition, other data that are used to benchmark the establishment survey suggest that monthly gains will eventually be revised down closer to a 200k/month pace, which is around our expectation of trend employment growth at today’s immigration levels.

Turning to the available leading indicators for June, the level of initial jobless claims between the May and June payroll survey week rose by 13k, and the four-week average of claims increased by 23k, although some of this may reflect residual seasonality. The all-industry PMI employment index jumped to a nine-month high of 51.7 after low readings in April and May, while among regional Fed surveys in the services industry (Dallas, KC, Philadelphia, and Richmond) the average employment index fell from 7.9 to 3.9.

Also in the establishment survey we forecast that the average workweek was unchanged at 34.3, and that average hourly

earnings increased 0.3%m/m. The increase in earnings would cause the year-over-year rate to continue to gradually trend down, to 3.9% in June from 4.1% in May, consistent with moderating inflation and a small amount of labor market easing, as measured by the gentle rise in the unemployment rate and the reduction in job openings.

In the household survey, the key question is whether the unemployment rate will continue to edge up. Last month was a low-side 4.0% (3.96%) and we expect that to one decimal point the rate will be unchanged. We also think the participation rate was stable at 62.5%. Participation had steadily increased through the end of last year and has since hovered between 62.5% and 62.7%.

## Consumer confidence (Jun 25)

<table><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Conference Bd index</td><td>97.5</td><td>102.0 101.3</td><td></td><td>102.0 100.4</td></tr><tr><td>Present situation</td><td>140.6</td><td>143.1 140.8</td><td></td><td>141.5</td></tr><tr><td>Jobs plentiful</td><td>38.4</td><td>37.5 37.0</td><td></td><td>38.1</td></tr><tr><td>Jobs hard to get</td><td>15.5</td><td>13.5 14.3</td><td></td><td>14.1</td></tr><tr><td>Labor mkt diff</td><td>22.9</td><td>24.0</td><td>22.7</td><td>24.0</td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>68.8</td><td>74.6</td><td>74.9</td><td>73.0</td></tr></table>

The Conference Board Consumer Confidence index softened slightly in June, falling to 100.4 from 101.3 in May, though household assessments of current conditions, including labor market conditions improved. In particular, the labor market differential, which correlates with the unemployment rate and is defined as the share of respondents reporting jobs are plentiful less the share reporting they are hard to get, improved a bit to 24.0% in June, compared to 22.7% in May and 22.9% in April. The improvement in the labor market differential does not preclude a further weakening in the unemployment rate in June--for example an increase in the unemployment rate to 4.1% from 4.0% would be possible at this level of the labor market differential--but the fact that the differential improved month-over-month was certainly positive and our forecast for June unemployment is for it to remain at 4.0%. The differential is weaker than in 1Q, when it averaged 30.4%, but is little different from the 4Q average of 24.7%.

Looking into the details, the present situations index rose to 141.5 in June from 140.8 in April while the expectations index declined to 73.0 from 74.9. While the expectations index remains low by historical standards it has generally been depressed for much of the last two years, and the latest reading is still within that range. Likewise, the present situations index is also still within the range of the last couple of years. Both measures are weaker than pre-pandemic, potentially reflecting the inflation to date since then. The Conference Board has noted a number of times that write-in responses show the rise in food costs in particular being a top concern for households.

Elsewhere in the survey one-year inflation expectations slipped back to 5.3%, down from 5.4% in May but are equal to their value in March and April. These are up a bit from the pre-pandemic levels (2019 average was 4.6%) but are not high by the standards of the mid-2000s to mid-2010s. Homebuying plans within the next six months were stable at 5.2%, still quite low.

## New home sales

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Mar</td><td colspan="2">Apr</td><td colspan="2">May</td></tr><tr><td>Total (000s,saar)</td><td>665</td><td>684</td><td>634 698</td><td>630</td><td>619</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>-5.4</td><td>6.4</td><td>-4.7</td><td>2.0</td><td>-0.6 -11.3</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>3.2</td><td>4.8</td><td>-8.3 3.3</td><td></td><td>15.0-16.4</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply</td><td>8.5</td><td>8.2</td><td>9.1</td><td>8.1</td><td>9.3</td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.5</td><td>3.9</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.9</td></tr></table>

New home sales declined substantially in May, down 11.3%m/m to a 619k annualized pace, but the blow was cushioned by material upward revisions to the prior three months, with the Feb-Apr average revised up 5% and the April reading revised up 10%, to 698k from 634k. This leaves 2Q tracking down at a 3.7%q/q annualized pace after a 11.4% increase in 1Q. The May reading is the lowest since November 2022, but especially with the upward revisions it would be too early to conclude that new home sales are breaking lower on a sustained basis, especially given that the NAHB homebuilder survey is still moving within its recent range and mortgage purchase applications (which also include the larger existing home market) are stable at a low level.

In terms of the signal for construction activity, recent sales and inventory figures could portend some further near-term softness. With data through May single-family starts are down at a 22%q/q annualized pace in 2Q after being almost flat in 1Q. Although starts are declining faster than sales this quarter, the ratio of starts to sales in recent months is still somewhat elevated, and over the last year the 3m moving average of starts is still up 13% while sales are down 3%.

Meanwhile inventory continued to increase last month, up 1.5%m/m and 12.9%oya to a new cycle high, which along with the decline in sales caused months’ supply to increase from 8.1 to 9.3, the highest since Oct 2022. One positive here is that the share of inventory that has already been completed, while steadily trending up, is still, at 21%, lower than prepandemic levels (2015-2019 average was 23%).

Median sale prices continued to move mostly sideways (-1%oya) as has been the case for the last couple years given strains on affordability. While affordability is also stretched for existing homes, new home sales continue to do fairly well relative to existing sales, given the still limited supply coming from the existing market. New sales were 14.3% of all singlefamily sales in May, down a bit from late last year but otherwise an elevated level (2015-2019 average of 11.1%). Homebuilders can also support sales through mortgage rate buydowns . To the extent these are being used, homebuilders can help offset them via factors like still high prices and lower interest expense amortization, and margins for a number of publicly traded builders are still robust.

## Durable goods (Jun 27)

<table><tr><td colspan="6">%m/m, sa</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td></td><td>May</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>0.8</td><td>-0.6</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.8</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Ex transportation</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.4</td><td></td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>-0.2</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.6</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>0.1</td><td>1.2</td><td></td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.4</td><td></td><td>ゆ</td><td>-0.5</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1</td><td></td><td></td><td>0.3</td></tr></table>

The capital goods details of the May durable goods report were on the soft side. Core capital goods shipments (nondefense ex aircraft) declined 0.5%m/m and are down at a 1.9%q/q saar pace so far in 2Q, while core capital goods orders fell 0.6%m/m. On a 12-month basis both core capital goods shipments and orders are roughly unchanged. Moreover, the capex intensions data that are posted in the regional Fed surveys suggest that there may not be much if any growth in core shipments in the coming months, although the relationship between these surveys and the shipments numbers is somewhat loose.

Overall durable goods shipments decreased 0.3%m/m while orders were up 0.1%m/m. Total shipments and orders ex transportation both declined 0.1%m/m. Ex transportation shipments are tracking a 0.7%q/q saar increase in 2Q, while total shipments are up at a 6.8% pace, reflecting 48%q/q saar growth in volatile civilian aircraft shipments. We still expect 8%q/q annualized equipment investment in 2Q GDP, stemming from gains in transportation spending as well as imported capital goods.

Inventories continued to track upward last month at the very steady pace we’ve seen since mid-2022, with another 0.3%m/ m increase in May. The ongoing growth is heavily concentrated in aircraft: including aircraft total durable inventories have risen 1.5%oya, but ex aircraft they are down 0.2%.

While this is a new low, the most recent reading is a just a bit below other recent observations. The May PCE report showed soft inflation and a real consumption trajectory for 2Q that is similar to the 1.5%q/q saar pace that we got in 1Q. On inflation, the core PCE index rose 0.1%m/m (0.08%) which brought the year-over-year rate down to 2.6% (2.57%) from 2.8%. Core services ex housing was also only up 0.1%m/m (3.4%oya), and headline inflation was 0.0%m/m (2.6%oya). The latest price data is good news for the Fed, though as FOMC members have noted they will want to see more low readings to gain confidence that inflation is moving back to 2%. Over the last three months core inflation rose 2.7% saar, down from a recent peak of 4.5% in March. While consistent with the story that the firm inflation we saw in 1Q was a bump amidst a gradual moderation, the May month-on-month change probably overstates how quickly core price growth will drop.

Real consumption rose 0.3%m/m (0.26%) in May and we now anticipate the eventual full quarter consumption will be around 1.7%q/q saar. Growth for the first quarter had originally been reported at 2.0% but after yesterday’s final GDP report that had been revised down to 1.5%. 1H24 consumption growth around 1.5% is a notable downshift from 2H23, when growth ran a touch over 3%.

Income growth was solid on the month, with nominal personal income increasing 0.5%m/m and compensation up 0.6%m/ m. With overall prices flat during the month real DPI increased a robust 0.5%m/m, the strongest gain we’ve seen over the past year. For the quarter though real DPI is increasing at a more mild 1.4% annualized pace, similar to the 1.3% gain in 1Q. The personal saving rate edged up to 3.9% in May from 3.7% in April though this is still a low reading.

<table><tr><td colspan="2">Consumer sentiment (Jun 28)</td><td colspan="2"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>May</td><td>Pre Jun</td><td>Fin Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Univ. of Mich. Index (nsa)</td><td>69.1</td><td>65.6</td><td>65.0 68.2</td></tr><tr><td>Current conditions</td><td>69.6</td><td>62.5</td><td>65.9</td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td>68.8</td><td>67.6</td><td>69.6</td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Short-term</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.0</td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.1</td><td>3.0</td></tr></table>

The University of Michigan consumer sentiment index was revised up to 68.2 in the final Jun report from 65.6 in the preliminary report, though it remains quite weak and is down from 69.1 in May and a recent high of 79.4 in March. The upward revision was in both current economic conditions and expectations, and the expectation index now shows a slight rise relative to May. As a reminder, the survey is switching from phone to web surveys in April through July, which the University of Michigan has said could reduce headline sentiment by about 1.6 points per month, with more of this concentrated in current economic conditions. This could thus explain a little more than 40% of the decline since March, though the Michigan press release has noted that month-onmonth trends, such as an index rising or falling from the prior month, is evident in both sets of responses.

Inflation expectations were revised lower in the final June report compared to the preliminary report, with one-year expectations cut from 3.3% to 3.0% and five-year expectations from 3.1% to 3.0%. One-year expectations are back near their post-pandemic low of 2.9% while remaining at the upper range of readings from the years immediately before the pandemic. The Michigan press release noted that respondents continue to express concern about higher prices, which is also a feature of the Conference Board’s consumer confidence survey although that survey has been consistently stronger than the Michigan one. Even with inflation moderating and a solid macroeconomic backdrop, it is possible that sentiment will remain depressed for a time as households acclimate to the higher level of prices.

## Focus: Potholes in the residential investment road

The May housing starts that were published last week pointed to current-quarter weakness in new residential construction activity, a theme that was further emphasized in this week’s data on May new single-family home sales. While sales of new homes continue to hold up better than sales of existing homes, the level of housing starts relative to sales suggests that home builders may further slow starts in the coming months, which could in turn leave single-family residential investment spending down at a double-digit annualized pace in the second half of the year. This assumes that sales remain near current levels, which would likely be too conservative an assumption if mortgage rates come down or if builders offer larger discounts. However, for the moment, June indicators such as the NAHB housing market index and mortgage purchase applications are remaining at subdued levels (Figure 1).

Figure 1: NAHB homebuilder survey
![](images/df89999e2863da558dda5a1561276eb358bb49d2f74afb4c7552125d516a0332.jpg)

The May data show new single-family housing starts declining at a 22%q/q annualized pace in 2Q, while sales are only down 4%. However, while new construction is already dropping faster than sales, construction has more room to decline. New home inventory is at a cycle high and is still moving up, and both the months’ supply of new homes (Figure 2) and ratio of starts to sales (Figure 3) is somewhat elevated.

Figure 2: New single-family homes - months' supply Ratio, sa
![](images/6162ae295be98ffe754c68973060d7ffb43c1094d8194f7ba3231edf4ca3869e.jpg)

Figure 3: Single-family starts built for sale relative to sales Ratio, sa, 3m avg
![](images/4576629b69e908884e197fca00e67916b5dbe21b3a2c932063b7bbbc4bd5b8ec.jpg)

To gauge the implications for residential investment, we assume that builders will bring the ratio of starts to sales down to a more normalized level. We assume this occurs over a six-month horizon, and consider the case where sales remain equal to their May level (619k annualized) as well as the three-month moving average (667k). On a quarterly basis the Census Bureau reports the fraction of starts that are being built for sale, with the 1Q ratio just over 75% on a seasonally adjusted basis, which is broadly in line with pre-pandemic levels. Assuming this fraction of starts being built for sale stays constant, then starts in the three months to May were roughly 15% higher than sales. This ratio has moved around a lot recently (it was over 20% earlier in the year), but it was reasonably stable between 2013 and 2019, when it averaged closed to 6%. Consequently, we assume that for a given path of sales that builders cut the ratio of starts to sales to 6% over six months. For simplicity, we assume that starts not being built for sale remain constant, as they are not closely correlated to starts for sale.

To translate this into a forecast for residential investment, we note that growth in the two-quarter moving average of singlefamily starts moves closely in line with quarterly changes in real single-family residential investment (Figure 4). In the case where new home sales hold at their three-month average, this would imply that 2H24 real single-family residential investment would be -14% annualized. If instead new home sales held at the lower May value, then the average growth would fall to -22%.

Figure 4: Single-family starts and real residential investement %q/q, saar

## Euro Area

• National data to a more modest increase in Euro area core prices of around 0.25%m/m, sa

EC economic sentiment stable in June, but broadly aligned with June PMI’s softer growth signal

• German pay news constructive at the margin

• New fiscal rules imply modest tightening next year

As France heads to the polls on Sunday in the first round of voting in the legislative elections, the outcome remains very hard to call. Polls continue to show a three-way split between the far-right RN, the NFP (left alliance) and Macron’s Ensemble (ENS), with RN poised to win the most votes. Translating this into seats remains hard as that will depend on how the contests shape up for the second round, including how many candidates make it through in each constituency. A relative majority for RN remains most likely in the second round, but this makes it hard to see how a government can be formed. A technocratic government could result, but would in many ways be unprecedented. This also makes the process of deciding on and passing a 2025 budget unclear. While all of this creates uncertainty, the RN has dropped many costly measures from its program and has said that it would respect the EU’s new fiscal rules.

In terms of the data, this week’s surveys followed the flash PMI lower, although with consumer sentiment still edging higher and the German labour market remaining solid. Hence, near-term nervousness about growth has increased, centred on manufacturing, but with key consumer fundamentals still intact. On inflation, linking this week’s flash national CPI releases to next week’s Euro area HICP estimate requires heroic assumptions, not just because Germany reports only on Monday. But, national data look in line with core prices rising 0.24%m/m, sa in June, which would be a tentative but encouraging slowing from the start of the year. And with growth having softened in June, that should keep the ECB on track for a September cut.

## Surveys raise manufacturing concerns

After last week’s 1.3pt decline in the flash composite PMI, this week’s surveys broadly confirmed that message. The EC economic sentiment survey was broadly unchanged in June, but its level is consistent with the 1% growth signal from the June composite PMI. The German IFO also matched the weakness of the PMI. By sector, the surveys continue to show relative resilience in services, which would align with a solid near-term outlook for household real incomes. That reflects still strong wage growth and a slowing already seen in headline inflation. The larger concern in the surveys is that manufacturing activity appears to have weakened more sharply and from already weak levels. In Germany, the message of weakness in manufacturing is consistent across the IFO and PMI (Figure 1). The reasons for this are not entirely clear as any uncertainty created by the French election would likely have had an impact on services as well.

Figure 1: German manufacturing surveys
![](images/da23e6b77a7dba0a2fc4bac102dd8dbd37d47a6f01bf18d7bad284fbe929cf31.jpg)

## Data-wrap up

Other data this week have provided partial updates on a range of topics.

Bank lending remains soft. This week’s M3 report showed broadly stagnant bank lending to nonfinancial corporates are only a slight increase to households. External financing needs remain low however given strong saving positions, so that conclusions for overall economic growth harder to reach.

French consumers perk up ahead of election uncertainty. French consumption of goods jumped 1.5%m/m in May, taking the April/May average to 0.6%ar above the 1Q24 level. But, unless there is large payback in May, the quarterly tracking could still improve with the June data. The big wildcard is the possibility of election-related uncertainty holding back some spending decisions.

German pay deals modestly constructive. This week’s deal for the German chemical sector will see pay rise 3.8% in 2024 and 2.4% in 2025. The recent construction deal was firmer, with both sectors facing economic challenges. But, we continue to see pay deals moderating once they have responded to the past inflation surge. In this sense, the incoming information remains consistent with a step-back in pay growth next year.

German labor market remains resilient. German unemployment rose and vacancies fell in June, but there was also another increase in employment. The latter is consistent with the business surveys, which also signal a roughly 0.5%ar on jobs growth. Businesses continue to report high levels of labor shortages, which is likely still acting to give solid employment growth relative to the growth of GDP.

## New fiscal rules begin to bite

While the snap election has put the focus on the French fiscal policy, adjustments are also necessary in some other Euro area countries. However, the exact adjustments depend on the new fiscal rules and how they are applied.

Figure 2 illustrates how the rules categorize countries into different groups and how this affects the required fiscal adjustments they have to make. The starting point is whether a country has a deficit persisting above -3% of GDP. Last week, the European Commission proposed opening Excessive Deficit Procedures (EDP) for France, Italy, Belgium, Slovakia and Malta. These countries would need to adjust their structural primary positions by 0.5% of GDP from next year until their deficits are below -3%.

![](images/e4f006bdef93f8d442547aab5f5b266b9090315b462b74a88c51825726bfeeb1.jpg)
Source: European Commission, J.P. Morgan

At that point, they would join the rest of the region in the preventative arm of the new rules. This puts countries into three groups. Those with low debt need to adjust to a structural deficit of -1.5%, those with 60%-90% debt need to reduce it by on average 0.5% of GDP per year and those with above 90% debt need to reduce it by on average 1% per year. The required adjustments are then translated into a new indicator, the Net Primary Expenditure, although in the end the old parameters still determine the debt trajectory (e.g. , the interest rate path, the structural primary position, one-offs and the stock-flow adjustment).

Based on the latest European Commission forecasts, five countries are meeting all criteria: the Netherlands, Luxembourg, Ireland, Estonia and Lithuania (Table 1). They must simply maintain a “prudent” debt trajectory”. Germany’s debt is a bit above 60% of GDP, but it is falling fast enough and

the structural deficit is low (although not quite low enough for the domestic debt brake). Debt in Greece, Portugal and Cyprus is also falling quickly, implying no adjustment need. While debt is projected to rise in Finland and Estonia, this relates to economic weakness and requires little or no structural adjustment.

Other countries will likely have to make some adjustment. Croatia and Latvia have low debt but need to adjust their structural positions, likely by 0.25% per year. Debt in Slovenia is falling, but the structural position likely requires a 0.25% per year adjustment. Austria also faces a 0.25% structural adjustment. In Spain, debt is falling by almost 1% of GDP per year, but this is due to strong growth rather than due to a low structural deficit, likely requiring an adjustment initially of possibly a bit more than 0.25% of GDP per year.

Source: AMECO, J.P. Morgan

Overall, our prior assumption of a 0.5% fiscal tightening next year continues to look reasonable. This leans quite heavily on Germany adjusting modestly for domestic reasons and, most importantly, the countries that are likely to go into EDPs having to make larger adjustments. But, some other countries are also likely to contribute to the overall tightening. There is some uncertainty about how quickly the required changes will be phased in and how strictly the -1.5% target under the preventative arm will be enforced for lower debt countries. But, overall a noticeable fiscal tightening will weigh on growth to some extent next year.

The Euro area composite PMI weakened abruptly in the flash report, reducing its signal for GDP growth from a 1.5%ar to a 1.0%ar. It is possible that the announcement of a snap election in France played a role as it was mentioned by survey participants in France. But, the PMI also weakened significantly outside of France. That manufacturing fell particularly sharply suggests also that other factors may have played a role. The final report will mostly reveal new details outside of Germany and France, but it will also be interesting to see if the Euro area aggregate sees revisions.

The flash Euro area composite PMI implied a 1.3pt decline outside of Germany and France. Our inclination is to think that both Italy and Spain were affected by this. However, the flash reports also implied that most of this was due to manufacturing output (-3.5pt) rather than services (-0.5pt). If Italian manufacturing saw that kind of decline, it would take it a worryingly low level. Hence, we have dampened that part modestly.

Germany orders have show some signs of stabilization, although this remains tentative due to the noise in the data and, more important, due to the weakness of the business surveys. The new orders index of the German manufacturing PMI had increased around 14pts in recent months to 46.6 in May but then dropped around 4pts in June. Hence, it is still at an improved level, but the next PMI report will be key to getting a sense of the trajectory. And, for now, it leaves the new orders index still signaling contraction in orders at around a 5%ar. For this reason, we have pencilled in a cautious forecast for May, which (strictly speaking) came just before the June decline in the PMI.

German IP got off to a surprisingly positive start to 2Q24 in April. While the output index of the German manufacturing PMI was still signalling contraction, the April level of manufacturing output was 2.4%ar above the 1Q24 average. There was a weather-related drag from construction, which saw payback after a large jump in 1Q24, but total IP was also still tracking a gain. In terms of leading indicators, the VDA data declined sharply in May after a strong outturn in April. And while the PMI improved in May it fell again in June and therefore continues to point to an underlying trend of contraction. We have penciled in a decline of 1.2%m/m, with risks possibly even skewed to the downside.

A number of countries have released June preliminary inflation estimates ahead of the Euro area flash HICP due on Tuesday. After these outcomes, we are still tracking a 2.5%oya Euro area headline forecast for June, which would represent a one-tenth decline compared to May.

The key question relates to core inflation, which increased two tenths to 2.9%oya in May for the Euro area. The country data for June are very limited and generally available in CPI terms, which is hard to translate in HICP terms. But these outcome overall are aligned with our view that Euro area core inflation likely remained stable at 2.9%oya in June.

Demand and labor markets

<table><tr><td>Unemployment stat)</td><td colspan="5"></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Harmonized measure (Euro-</td><td>Feb</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Tue Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Jul 2</td><td>Unemployment rate (%, sa)</td><td>6.5</td><td>6.5</td><td>6.4</td><td>6.4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Unemployment (ch m/m,</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>11:00am</td><td>000s)</td><td>21.0</td><td>-105.0</td><td>-100.0</td><td>-50</td></tr></table>

The Euro area unemployment rate is likely to have remained unchanged in May, but we suspect that the number of unemployed continued to decline. This would be consistent with the business surveys, which have shown strong hiring in recent months, including after the setback in the June surveys. In part, this reflects ongoing weakness in measured output per worker, with employment continuing to look firm relative to the growth in GDP.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Retail sales</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td></td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Fri</td><td>Euro area</td><td>Feb</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jul 5</td><td>Sales ex. autos and petroleum, volumes, sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>11:00am</td><td>%m/m</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.7</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>%oya</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.0</td><td></td></tr></table>

Euro area retail sales may have been stable in May, although with April likely to get revised upwards to around -0.2%m/m. The latter is suggested by the earlier upward revision in Germany. If correct, it would leave Euro area retail sales tracking gain of around 1.5% annualized so far in 1Q24, which would be consistent with a support from rising real incomes have an impact on goods spending as well as services spending.

<table><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td>Feb Mar</td><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">By Jul 5 Germany</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td>Sales ex. autos and petroleum, volumes, sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">%m/m</td><td>-1.4</td><td>2.6</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td colspan="2">%oya</td><td>-2.2</td><td>1.1</td><td>0.4</td><td></td></tr></table>

As is often the case, German retail sales are reported weak and then revised up in the regular mid-month revision. This was the case again this time, with April now reported almost stable and its level 4.4%ar above 1Q24. German disposable income was slower to pick up than elsewhere in real terms, but is likely rising solidly now. This is consistent with the strength in recent negotiated pay data, reflecting the timing of pay deals. From this perspective, German retail sales are likely to perform better going forward than the recent trend. But, then April looks strong relatively to 1Q24 already. Balancing these considerations, we expect a small increase in May.

Source: European Commission, Eurostat, ECB, FSO, Bundesbank, IFO, INSEE, ISAE, Istat, INE, CBS, BNB, Markit, and J.P. Morgan forecasts

## Review of past week’s data

Output and surveys

<table><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td></td><td>Jun</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>% balance of responses, sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td>96.1</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Economic confidence</td><td>95.6</td><td></td><td>96.0</td><td>96.1</td><td>95.9 -10.1</td></tr><tr><td>Industrial confidence Construction confidence</td><td>-10.4 5.9</td><td>-6.0</td><td>-9.9 6</td><td>-6.2</td><td>-7.0</td></tr><tr><td>Retail confidence</td><td>-6.8</td><td></td><td>-6.8</td><td></td><td>-7.8</td></tr><tr><td>Service confidence</td><td>6.1</td><td></td><td>6.5</td><td>6.8</td><td>6.5</td></tr><tr><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Consumer confidence</td><td>-14.7</td><td></td><td>-14.3</td><td>-14.0</td><td></td></tr></table>

The EC economic sentiment survey declined 0.2pt to 95.9 in June. This level is aligned with the latest PMI reading, which declined significantly last week and is consistent with a 1.0% ar pace of activity in June. Surveys overall are consistent with our 1.5% ar 2Q GDP forecast and put downside risks around our 3Q 1.5% ar GDP forecast. Looking at the details, the EC survey has moved sideways in recent months with two important dynamics: the rise in consumer confidence (on the back of strong real wage gains) has been offset by a decline in

business confidence. In level terms, however, both stand below their historical average. Looking at the business details, sentiment is still significantly stronger in services than in other sectors (manufacturing, retail and construction). And looking at the country details, sentiment remains weakest in Germany. An important aspect of the EC release we have been highlighting is price intentions indicators. The aggregate reading increased a little in June, but the survey still hints at a lower year-on-year reading for the GDP deflator as we move through the year (a measure of domestic inflation).

## National business surveys

<table><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td></td><td>Jun</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>German IFO survey</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>2015=100,sa</td><td></td><td></td><td>89.3</td><td>89.3</td><td>88.6</td></tr><tr><td>Business climate Business situation</td><td>89.3</td><td>89.4</td><td></td><td>88.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Business expectations</td><td>88.9 89.7</td><td>89.8</td><td>88.3 90.4 90.3</td><td>90.4</td><td>89.0</td></tr></table>

The German IFO weakened in June (-0.7pts to 88.6) due to a deterioration in business expectations (-1.3pts to 89.0), while current conditions were stable at 88.3. Directionally, the fall in IFO expectations matches the 1.8pt fall in last week’s composite PMI. But, in terms of level, the two surveys are still sending very different messages about GDP growth. The composite PMI’s GDP signal dropped from a 1.3%ar in May to a 0.7%ar in June, whereas the IFO expectations index has been unusually weak for two years now and remains in contraction territory. In our view, the PMI has performed better at tracking German GDP (see first chart) and therefore we put more weight on it. Hence, the German economy is still expanding, but at a pace that is now below our forecast of 1.25%q/q saar in 3Q24 and 0.75%q/q saar in 4Q24.

Despite a further increase in unemployment (+18,000) in June, the broader labour market data remain resilient. Vacancies are continuing to fall in terms of those registered with the Labour Agency, but are still much higher on the more comprehensive IAB measure, and employment is continuing to increase (+20,000 in May), suggesting that the increase in unemployment is due to employment not quite keeping pace with the growth of the labour force, rather than due to job cuts. Increases in employment are also consistent with the business surveys, which continue to show steady hiring, even with the declines in the June surveys. Separately, short-time work applications have slowed a bit, although the take-up has edged up to 62,000 workers in April on a full-time basis. Hence, there is some softness in the labour market data, but not in a way that changes the picture of overall resilience.

French consumption rose strongly in May, leaving goods consumption in Apr/May 0.6% annualized above the 1Q24 average. This is a significant improvement after a weak start into the current quarter. And, unless there is payback in June, the quarterly tracking is going to improve further. Political uncertainty may be a factor that could affect June however.
