## Financial activity and public finance

<table><tr><td>Money and credit data</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td></td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>Euro area</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>M3 (%m/m sa)</td><td>0.4</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>M3 (%oya)</td><td>0.9 0.8</td><td>1.3 0.9</td><td></td><td>1.6 0.8</td></tr><tr><td>Loans (%oya)1.</td><td>16.0</td><td>17.8</td><td>16.5</td><td>3.2</td></tr><tr><td>Loans (m/m, €bn)1.</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Of which</td><td>2.3</td><td>2.6</td><td>3.2</td><td>2.6</td></tr><tr><td>To households</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>To nonfinancial corporates 1. Loans to nonbank private sector, adjusted for securitization</td><td>4.7</td><td>3.4 44</td><td>-5.4</td><td>5.2</td></tr></table>

The ECB’s M3 report continued to show a weak trend in bank lending to the real economy through to May. Net lending to non-financial corporates rose by €5bn and lending to households rose by €3bn, neither of which changes the recent trends much. Hence, bank lending to non-financial corporates is close to stagnant, while lending to households is growing at a 0.5% annualised rate. Arguably, both sectors have decent net lending/saving positions and therefore a reduced need for external financing, with households also benefitting from a solid real income constellation (i.e. wage growth still solid and headline inflation having slowed down). And, interest rates on new loans have only edged down from their recent peaks through to April. According to the last ECB Bank Lending Survey from April, credit conditions are no longer being tightened, so that weakness on bank lending looks more related to demand.

## Japan

• BoJ June opinions suggest increasing confidence over policy normalization

• But slow-moving BoJ meets faster-moving capital outflows: currency pressure resumes

• Tokyo inflation firms through June; May activity reports point to recovery

• Next week: BoJ 2Q Tankan signals solid profits

We remain confident that the BoJ is on a gradual path toward monetary policy normalization that will see the policy rate move moderately higher over the next few quarters. Our baseline remains for a hike next month. And a gradual series of rate rises will, we think, be accompanied by a more material tightening in system liquidity as QT begins in earnest this summer. As the BoJ steps back from bond purchases, the trajectory for longer-end yields will become increasingly hostage to, and a reflection of, rising inflation expectations.

Alongside what has been a slow-moving BoJ thus far, we have also highlighted the risk of faster-moving capital outflows. Even as the BoJ normalizes policy, we continue to forecast an unprecedented period of negative real rates in Japan that can fuel shifts out of yen cash deposits and push up domestic asset price inflation, but also catalyze significant capital outflow and currency depreciation pressure. This fundamental backdrop looks unlikely to change materially—even with the yen having fallen to a post-1986 low against the USD of 160 this week, and even with some (incremental) convergence between the BoJ and the Fed in 2H24 (Figure 1).

Figure 1: JPY NEER and Japan household inflation expectations Jan 2004=100 % of respondents, 1y ahead
![](images/777aae3d580236efe4af067796288e19f44925c6df449875d8ffb384cf6d14c5.jpg)

With the yen’s slide once again front and center, this week’s summary of opinions suggests that the BoJ is gearing up for its next move. Several board members appear increasingly confident about the path of policy normalization, consistent with our forecast for an upcoming hike, likely to 25bp. Several opinions suggested that “upside risks to prices have become more noticeable,” with one arguing that the Bank “should raise the policy interest rate not too late.” Renewed

currency depreciation adds some urgency to these comments.

The data flow over the past week was largely in line with our expectations, with a rise in retail sales and production in May, and a pickup in underlying price momentum in the Tokyo CPI report for June. Next week, the focus will be on the BoJ’s June Tankan survey, which should show solid corporate capex and profit plans in the face of tepid manufacturing sentiment. The Cabinet Office is also expected to release an off-cycle revision to its 1Q GDP estimates, revising construction spending lower, and likely prompting another downgrade to growth. Given the backward-looking nature of the GDP data, we do not expect this revision to alter the BoJ’s policy path materially, even if it could prompt some adjustments to the BoJ’s own GDP forecasts.

## BoJ strikes confident tone

In the Bank’s June summary of opinions, the first on monetary policy, likely to have come from the Governor, stated that “the Bank will raise the policy interest rate and adjust the degree of monetary accommodation” if activity and prices move in line with views published in the April Outlook Report. This signal of an upcoming policy rate hike is a departure from the April meeting, where a similar statement noted only that the degree of policy accommodation could be adjusted, and even then, “accommodative financial conditions will be maintained.” This part was dropped in June.

On the outlook for inflation, there was a clear shift in tone among the Board. One member argued that the policy rate should only be raised once inflation had “started to rebound.” But several others noted the risk of moving too slowly given that “it has become easier for firms to pass on cost increases”; the “possibility that prices will deviate upward from the baseline scenario”; and “upside risks to prices have affected consumer sentiment.” Indeed, this latter member noted that data should be monitored “in preparation for the next MPM”—a clear reference to the potential for a hike in July.

One surprise was a direct hawkish reference to the neutral policy rate, with one Board member noting that “the appropriate, risk-neutral level of the policy interest rate should rise in reflection of the increased upside risks to prices.” This opinion was expressed in the context of yen depreciation; other members also saw the need to consider “whether further adjustments to monetary accommodation are needed.” These statements appear to be a signal that several members are now comfortable expressing views of a rate hike cycle, rather than a discreet one-off exit from unorthodox easing policies.

On balance sheet policy and QT, all opinions were consistent with a shift such that “long-term interest rates will be formed more freely in financial markets.” Several members expressed %3m/3m saar, dashed line is manufacturers' projection opinions along the lines that the Bank should proceed carefully. But the Board appears to want to signal that its mediumterm objective is for “a new [JGB] market structure” to emerge in which the BoJ is not dominant.

There were very few clues about the pace of reduction in bond purchases: one member noted cuts in buying should be “sizeable” while others emphasized the need for balance sheet reduction to be “predictable” and “smooth.”

## Tokyo CPI firms in June

Tokyo’s June core prices (ex. food and energy) rose 0.3%m/m sa, leaving global standard core inflation at 1.4%oya. This was a touch stronger than our forecast, and the other core inflation measures were similarly around 10bp higher than consensus expectations. The sequential trend in global core prices fell 0.1%3m/3m saar, the weakest pace in almost two years, but this is largely a reflection of free high school tuition fees introduced in April that are distorting services price aggregates. By contrast, non-education services prices firmed in June. Momentum in medical fees, the cost of eating out, and recreation services prices all accelerated on a 3m/3m basis (Figures 2 and 3).

Figure 2: Tokyo global core CPI (ex. food and energy) % chg.
![](images/2b3f9f1d15ccb60c0c0ceab021b2c6098cac3ac7276931d016d422df3985214a.jpg)

Figure 3: Tokyo services prices composition
![](images/6f1b20b23f70801ae5d3f8df1a2fad5c74c429e73c8bcb1d4f6dfbf8bac0de7e.jpg)
Source: Statistics Bureau, J.P. Morgan

On an over-year-ago basis, the BoJ’s official (2.1%) and preferred (1.8%) measures of core inflation also accelerated over their May rates. Official core CPI (ex. fresh food) inflation had trended down since early 2023, reflecting the impact of energy inflation subsidies. But with their removal, this trend is reversing, as we had expected. Energy prices were up 3.4%m/m sa in June, adding 0.4%-pts to official over-yearago core inflation.

The decision to reintroduce utility price subsidies from August through October will again temporarily depress energy’s contribution to headline and official core inflation rates. But we expect underlying core price momentum to reaccelerate on the back of firming nominal wage growth and a rising inflationary impulse from imported goods prices amid sustained yen depreciation. This should keep core inflation above the BoJ’s 2% target through 2H24, and keep the BoJ on track to deliver two more policy rate increases by year-end.

## Output rises as tech soars

Japan’s May industrial production rose 2.8%m/m sa, a touch stronger than our forecast and consensus expectations for a 2% rise. Momentum accelerated 7.8%3m/3m saar, its strongest pace in a year and a half. We had expected output to turn up in May as auto production bounced back before a second round of plant shutdowns commences going into early 3Q. Against this backdrop, producers expect a large near-5% fall in production in June, which would see momentum decelerate 4: Japan Manufacturing outputnext quarter (Figure 4). The risk at this point is thus that tem-<sub>m</sub> <sub>saar,</sub> <sub>dashed</sub> <sub>line</sub> <sub>is</sub> <sub>manufacturers'</sub> <sub>projection</sub>porary halts to auto production will see production throttle back next quarter.

Figure 4: Japan Manufacturing output
![](images/58779af33d82f15c90c7a7a9b8066073d5963aa1904efd6e26b7ba8ad24839e7.jpg)

Figure 5: Japan Electronic parts and devices
![](images/34c51ef8fefba1d5b1e19be9fe7ae2113b29cc73db96fe49ab6ab3512ca2b7b1.jpg)

Figure 6: Japan Employment by industry 2015=100, sa by J.P. Morgan
![](images/c5423a9b0f9ed07283a26586f011edb3f883e760529890c232ea4750f62d2869.jpg)

Aside from an 18%m/m sa pop in auto production, electronics output also rose a firm 2.3% in May, and expanded almost 35%3m/3m saar, a post-pandemic high. We think the sharp recovery in tech reflects both supply and demand dynamics. Alongside a regional tech cycle upswing, Japan’s tech sector is also benefitting from an expansion in supply of integrated circuits. Chip production firmed another 6.7%m/m sa after even larger rises earlier in the year, bringing the level of out put close to 30% over its average 2023 level (Figure 5).

May data continue to suggest some moderate negative spillovers into the labor market from a weak run in manufacturing, potentially related to the auto plant suspensions (Figure 6). The unemployment rate held steady at 2.6% sa in May for the fourth consecutive month, and measures of tightness continue to moderate; the jobs-to-applicants ratio edged down for the second month running.

## Retail sales pick up pace

May retail sales rose a stronger-than-expected 1.7%m/m sa, leaving spending up 4.3%3m/3m saar, its fastest pace in seven months. Real consumer spending has tended to come in softer than the nominal retail sales data, given sustained rises in prices. But the data are nonetheless signaling a moderate inflection higher in consumption momentum (Figure 7). This rise looks broadly in line with our forecast of 0.5%q/q saar rise in GDP private consumption this quarter following a near-3% fall in 1Q.

Last week’s decision to extend electricity, gas, and fuel subsidies should also mitigate some of the downside risk to activity we had seen from an expected jump in headline inflation, and which had already weighed on consumer sentiment (Figure 8). But details of the May spending data are not yet signaling a broad recovery in consumption. Strength was concentrated in a 8.3%m/m sa pop in auto purchases, reversing a near-5% drop in April. Spending on electronics, apparel, and food all posted monthly declines.

## Canada

• May CPI inflation surprised stronger...

• … but not because of CPI basket re-weighting

• June u-rate to keep rising despite solid job growth

The upside inflationary surprise in the May CPI report was the highlight of the week for Canadian data releases. Headline CPI rose 2.9% over the past year, up from 2.7%, when we and consensus had expected a further fall. The Bank of Canada’s preferred core measures also averaged 2.9% in May, the first rise in their year-ago paces since December. They now reside just inside of the BoC’s target range (Figure 1).

This all happened in the first CPI report after the BOC kicked off its easing cycle, just three weeks ago. While not adding confidence that underlying inflation is cooling, it also does not derail the easing cycle: progress toward price stability was always likely to be choppy and gradual, and the Governing Council acknowledged that upside risks to the inflation outlook remain. Our expectation remains for inflation to gradually cool further this year, but the May report also reinforces our expectation that the BoC will take a cautious approach, with policy decisions on a meeting-by-meeting basis. We look for the next 25bp rate cut to happen in 4Q this year.

Figure 1: BoC core CPI measures
![](images/26b27bebb34c5df5de0654dde7deba03d68424b164a8dfc1c63701995f6cd3bc.jpg)

## May CPI disappointed, surprisingly firm

The pickup in the CPI last month was largely the result of strong price growth for services, which rose 4.6% on a yearago basis in May. Strength in rental costs (8.9%oya) was an important contributor to the upside surprise and suggests that shelter could be a lingering source of persistence in inflation. Housing supply has struggled to keep up with housing demand, which has kept upward pressure on home prices and rents. Price growth for goods held steady at 1.0%oya in May.

Statistics Canada incorporated revised CPI basket weights based on 2023 expenditure data with the release of the May data, as has happened each May in recent years. While an upward revision to its weight boosted the contribution of another firm month for shelter costs in May (Figure 2), the updated weights were otherwise not a significant driver of the upside surprise in last month’s figures. Both headline and core CPI would still have been reported at 2.9%oya in May using the old basket weights.

Figure 2: Change in May CPI and basket weights May 2024; %m/m, nsa

Morgan Weight chg, Apr to May 2024; parts per 100</td></tr></table>

## A solid jobs report won’t stop rising u-rate

The May inflation data further complicate the picture for the BoC against a background of some signs that labor market is weakening. For example, those saying they were working part-time for economic reasons rose in May. Meanwhile, although job growth has been running strong—at around a 2% annual pace—it has not been able to keep pace with growth in the labor force, which has been averaging around 3% for the past year or so thanks in large part to strong immigration (Figure 3). As a result, the unemployment rate has been trending steadily higher since the start of 2023, and we look for it to tick up another tenth in next week’s June labor force report to 6.3%.

Figure 3: Employment and labor force growth %oya
![](images/b691c68fa8cd788679fded179eb18d81b6b8bba0519c67137e1805b1de8423d0.jpg)

A supportive supply side should help keep wage growth in check, offering some solace to the BoC on inflationary pressures—especially in the services sector. In the end, a solid job market also supports our expectation for a gradual easing cycle. But if the labor market were to show signs of a more material weakening, we would expect the BoC’s easing cycle to turn more aggressive.

## Mexico

• Banxico kept rates on hold in a non-unanimous decision

• Softer growth and benign core inflation warranted a dove stance

• We now expect Banxico to cut in August instead of September

• Incoming data for 2Q proving better than April’s but downside risks in place

Our call last month was still one in which benign core inflation would eventually align with a smooth political transition in the summer and a Fed factor that would not greatly affect the decision-making process from Banxico.

It turned out to be quite different. News of a legislative landslide from Morena and the risk of myriad constitutional antimarket reforms was followed by a less-confrontational speech from President-elect Sheinbaum. The market overshoot was followed by mixed dynamics in local markets that also were echoed in Banxico’s statement this week. Vigilance was warranted as the board continues to assess the reach of potential constitutional changes, but downside risks to growth and benign inflation dynamics figured prominently in the statement.

As such, the board kept rates on hold at 11% but suggested that the inflation outlook will allow to them consider rate cuts in the next meetings

Figure 1: Policy rate expectations
![](images/6995ac9fc1a2681f0b4bfedeaef644ad4d883aa3d2e9fbd563732129d62a797f.jpg)
Source: Banxico and J.P. Morgan forecasts. Banxico survey published June 6. Market pricing as of June 27 (labeled)

We believe there is only space for intermittent actions in 2H24 (Figure 1) considering the domestic and external geopolitical environment. As we note this week, core inflation is delivering encouraging news and with growth disappointing we believe the board could favor back-to-back cuts; however uncertainty related to political transitions in the region and delayed actions from the Fed will relevel the balance of risks for the Bank. We are maintaining our call for only two additional cuts this year, but now expect a 25bp cut in August instead of September, followed by a 25bp cut in December to 10.5% . We acknowledge the risk of another cut.

## Core composition finally in a good mix

The preliminary June CPI was roughly in line with expectations. Annual headline inflation picked up some to 4.8%, but core was stable at 4.2%. Beyond the aggregate numbers, there were two layers of positive news: core came in lower, and, more importantly, the composition of core inflation was very encouraging from a disinflation perspective. On this latter point, we are warming more to the idea that services inflation might be starting to slow more noticeably after a long spell of stickiness at very high rates.

Core inflation composition was better than earlier this year and points toward more of a trend improvement. Goods prices rose more than we expected, largely on account of a forceful rebound in nonfood goods. Still, overall goods prices point to a 0.23%m/m, sa increase in June, which is still a tenth below the pre-pandemic norm.

The most welcome development is the trend in services prices. Overall services were a bit softer than we expected despite firm housing and large gains in some tourism categories. However, when we strip out these categories and look at nontourism services (which also exclude education), prices were unchanged. Non-tourism price gains, which are least subject to swings related to unusual seasonality (leap-year, Easter), had been on a modest downtrend between Feb-Apr, but remained notably high. By contrast, non-tourism services slowed markedly in May and likely even further in June (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Core CPI - Non-tourism services
![](images/62054ed60cbad62621ba02427abafae81edc20fb1708a9305608424bb37930c8.jpg)
Source: INEGI and J.P. Morgan. June data is J.P. Morgan's forecast

Factoring in this week’s data into our forecast, non-tourism prices would have increased just 0.18%m/m, sa in June, well below the norm, and the smallest increase since March 2022. A word of caution: while restaurants are proving a short-term tailwind in recent months, the structural pressure on services inflation from rapid real wage growth (amid low productivity)

should remain in place. Also, recent raw-food upward pressures could exert additional pressure on core gauges (e.g. restaurants, processed food) if sustained.

## Net exports probably not a drag in 2Q

GDP growth has been very soft over the past few quarters, despite robust spending by both consumers and businesses. Net exports have generally been the culprit, as they’ve proved a strong drag on GDP growth. This has been a function of both weak external demand and surging imports. The mix of a strong consumer and a strong currency helps explain these dynamics. Things are probably starting to change in 2Q.

In May, factory shipments surged 3%m/m and what’s more, contrary to most of 2022-23 the increase was not driven solely by auto-related exports; this time around non-auto exports were up 3.6%m/m, with auto exports rising a solid 1.8%. Adding to our optimism from an external demand perspective, Mexico’s PMI data for June early next week could be more encouraging than the May gauges.

On the imports front, non-oil consumer and capital imports were soft. We expect capital imports to gradually soften on a more uncertain domestic outlook while consumer-good imports could soften as well if the MXN depreciation is sustained and long-lasting.

Finally, not a lot of emphasis has been made on an increasing drag for import growth, the systematic decline in oil-related purchases. These imports contracted roughly 50% annualized in 4Q23 and 1.5% in 1Q24. They are tracking a 40% drop in 2Q24. Price changes aside, the government’s push to substitute oil-related imports with domestic production seems to be gradually but steadily gaining traction.

## Transition update

Now that the federal election is nearly four weeks past and the dust has (partially) settled in terms of the uncertainty related to the implications of such a strong mandate for Morena, questions abound about the details of the incoming president’s agenda.

The main concern of Morena holding to a constitutional majority in the Lower House and two seats short in the Senate is the blank cheque granted to a party that has signaled, since 2018, its intentions of continuing with an agenda of powerconcentration and anchoring spending programs that could jeopardize public finances and curb investment in the next years. Particularly concerning is the pressure exerted on public finances given recurring transferences to Pemex (Figure 3).

Encouraging enough, though, Mrs. Sheinbaum has delivered a number of messages intended to signal a level field for the private sector on some important grounds, even if details are still unclear. Mixed and contrasting messages between outgoing president Lopez and Sheinbaum’s new team have blurred the picture but are expected to fade out as we near the start of the new ordinary session of Congress on September 1 and Sheinbaum’s swearing-in on October 1. In addition, the announcements of cabinet members have been well received by the market, including this week’s naming of Luz Elena González as energy minister.

Figure 3: Federal Government oil revenue versus transfers to Pemex US\$bn
![](images/555083993fcb18f41fd71c65cfbc0c8b3a2d70dde01418d63d9695dfec193513.jpg)

## Brazil

• COPOM reinforced hawkish tone

• But inflation forecasts do not suggest need to hike rates, for now

With bar too high for spending cuts and spending pressures, budget remains a concern

• Tax reform regulation may be voted in coming weeks

This was one of those weeks with multiple official communications important for monetary policy. The tone was hawkish but even if BCB is keeping all cards on the table, it is avoiding signaling rate hikes for now. We continue looking for stability in the policy rate ahead, recognizing that the exchange rate depreciation puts the central bank in an increasingly uncomfortable position.

There were many tweaks to the communication that justify the central bank’s interruption of the cycle. BCB changed its GDP growth forecast from 1.9% to 2.3%, closer to our longstanding 2.2% estimate, amid a tight labor market and an output gap close to neutrality. It also changed the neutral rate to 4.75%, a more cautious assumption given the median of its own models at 5%. BCB sees the pace of disinflation is stalling, with the behavior of the labor market and aggregate demand becoming increasingly important from now on. Against this backdrop, a“minority” of members see the balance of risks as asymmetric to the upside, indicating that the central bank could react if the outlook deteriorates further.

Yet, there has been a notable absence of a debate about policy rate hikes by any of the Board members. BCB is also projecting inflation very close to the target already next year assuming a stable Selic rate, and avoided showing the 2026 CPI estimates that are likely below the target in this scenario. The prospects for 2026 inflation will become increasingly important in the central bank’s relevant horizon for monetary policy as we enter 2H24. These choices suggest BCB disavows the recent market pricing of rate hikes starting this year.

In the meantime, the National Monetary Council (CMN) confirmed the 3% inflation target, and the government issued a decree abandoning the inflation target by calendar-year, favoring a continuous target, with an innovation of establishing that any change on the mid-target will only take effect after 36 months. The decree is unlikely to significantly change the central bank’s reaction function, a positive development, in our view. This could help to stop the deterioration of inflation expectations, though the government’s hesitancy in recognizing the need for spending cuts is concerning. At this point we estimate the need for at least BRL15-20bn in spending freezes, as well as additional measures on revenues and expenditures eyeing the 2025 budget. Failure to do so would likely contribute to the souring mood by reinforcing the perception that the fiscal rules will not be fully observed until the end of the current presidential mandate.

## Tax reform update

On a more structural note, both Lula and Finance Minister Haddad pointed out the importance of the constitutional tax reform approved last year for growth. The next few weeks will be critical as the Lower House negotiates the terms of two bills to regulate the transition to the new system, set to start in 2026 and stretch until 2032. The debate around the basic food staples list, the excise tax and how the new system will work from an operational standpoint – including the decision on cash back to poor families – are the main topics to monitor. Speaker Arthur Lira has vowed to have the bill approved in the Lower House before the mid-year recess (July 18), which means that initial drafts may surface as early as next week. The proposal will still need to be approved in Senate before moving to presidential sanction.

## Core services downshifted in CPI preview

IPCA-15 (CPI preview) grew 0.39%m/m, nsa, below the consensus of 0.44% and well below our call of 0.50%, and core CPI rose 0.34%m/m, nsa, 12bp below our expectations. Seasonally adjusted BCB's core was consistent with 3.5%m/m, saar, driving the 3-month average up a tenth to 3.3%3m/3m, saar, with core services below 4% for the second month in a row, bringing the 3-month average to 4%, the lowest level since the downward blip in 3Q23. Core goods CPI rose at the fastest pace in almost a year, particularly due to higher vehicle prices, with its run rate now back around the mid-point of the inflation target (Figure 1). We still look for inflation to end the year at 4%.

Figure 1: Core IPCA-15 (CPI preview)
![](images/73afe760b74f5c6f57e0fcd30a514866787dbfc1005c726a1e2738add0a694cd.jpg)

## Credit grew and unemployment fell in May

The unemployment rate moved down by 30bp to 6.9% SA, just a couple of tenths off the lows of the last 12 years. The average real wage bill gained 0.5%m/m, sa in May, with nominal wages accelerating from 9%oya to 9.7%, and real wages from 4.7% to 5.6%. The other metrics are running below those levels, but all accelerating in May (Figure 2).

Current account balance
Figure 2: Wage growth: different measures
![](images/1ce3884fb461996c97f6a08feff03a40f02477b174342b2ff48ca2eeb6df270b.jpg)

New loans grew 1.0% in May according to BCB, reflecting a 1.0% marginal contraction for enterprises and an expansion of 2.0% for families. The stock of outstanding credit grew for households and enterprises, accelerating to 8.0%oya and 5.1% in real terms, respectively. Interest rates fell for the second consecutive month after a brief hike in March but NPLs and arrears increased a tenth to 3.3% and 4.1% at the aggregate level. Household indebtedness also increased one-tenth in April, with average income commitment of 26.6% (increasing 0.4%-pt, but still 1.4%-pts lower in 12 months).

## CAD moves up to 1.8% of GDP

The current account deficit (CAD) widened again after registering a deficit of US\$3.4 billion in May, increasing twotenths to 1.8% of GDP over the month. BCB announced that the treatment of crypto asset net imports will be changed in the next release in July, which could reduce the reported deficit by 0.7%-pt (Figure 3). The foreign direct investment (FDI) disappointed expectations by registering inflows of US\$3.0 billion – equivalent to 3% of GDP. Portfolio flows were positive, with a US\$2.2 billion inflow to fixed income.

Figure 3: Current account balance
![](images/68bd60d00f04c993b6726c7a73d3cce65e4a5d1bbd5745be3e80ec005fa15526.jpg)

## Fiscal results command policy decision

The general government posted a higher-than-expected BRL63.9bn deficit in May, pushing net public debt to 62.2% of GDP – the highest level on record. Marginal results reinforce concerns with fiscal policy credibility, raising questions about the spending limit and the targets. Ahead, we will monitor the political disposition to face such challenges, acknowledging that it will require more than words for us to maintain our moderate 0.8% deficit forecasts for 2024 and 2025.

## Argentina

• Omnibus bill and fiscal package fully approved in Congress after six months of negotiations

• 1Q24 GDP plunged by 9.9%q/q, saar

• Economic activity to stabilize in 2Q24

• 1Q24 (accrued) current account posted a US\$240mn surplus

• We revise lower 2024 accrued current account surplus to US\$4bn

After six months of intense negotiations, the government finally achieved the approval of the long-awaited Omnibus bill and fiscal package. With the support of PRO, some members of UCR and Hacemos Coalición Federal, the ruling party obtained 147 positive votes at the Lower House to finally sanction the Omnibus Bill. In the case of the fiscal package, the government also managed to reach the simple majorities needed to reinstate the income tax and the changes in personal goods tax, securing 0.5% of GDP in additional fiscal revenues.

Following the approval of the Omnibus bill and fiscal package, the government celebrated the legislative victory and formally called governors, former presidents, leaders of the main political parties and legislators to join them on July 9 in Tucumán for the signing of the “May Pact” with 10 foundational policies to bring “greatness to the Nation”.

In a TV interview, President Milei emphasized they are working to lift FX controls and anticipated that a change in the monetary regime is the next step to follow, though a set of conditions (including finding a solution to the Treasury’s debt put options) are first needed to advance on this front. In addition, the president ratified MinFin Caputo’s promise to reduce the “Impuesto Pais” tax, though clarified this will be done once the Omnibus bill and fiscal package are regulated and new revenues start flowing, likely by August. Finally, the president emphasized the roadmap includes additional structural reforms, and confirmed Sturzenegger will be joining the cabinet next week as minister in charge of executing the structural reform agenda.

## Consumption and investment plummeted

The 1Q24 national accounts report showed sequential GDP plunging by 9.9%q/q, saar, less of a contraction that what the monthly economic activity indicator had suggested (-11.5%ar). Yet, the contraction in 4Q23 was revised to 9.6%ar, from 7.4%. Therefore, despite the sequential growth revisions, the GDP level came in roughly in line with expectations. Indeed, real GDP has lost 5.1%, sa, compared to the cyclical peak of 1Q23, a contraction that is not far from the -6.4% experienced in between 4Q18 and 4Q17, when the economy was shocked by a sudden stop to capital flows (Figure 1).

![](images/8acdb944d97681123f69346c1217a196951a59f2b7bef1b8f81c2d966f5d1b20.jpg)

The demand side breakdown helps to gauge the main factors behind the recession. On the one side, the front-loaded macroeconomic adjustment drastically impacted domestic demand in the first quarter of the year. Fixed investment collapsed by 42%ar, reaching 22% below the recent peak as of 2Q23. On its side, private consumption plummeted 10.0%ar, and is now 5.7% below the peak observed in 1Q23. But despite the fiscal adjustment, the national accounts showed public consumption ‘only’ down by 3.2%ar in 1Q24. When compared to the recent peak as of 2Q23, public consumption is down by 5.2%, sa—that is, by less than the compression in private consumption (Figure 2). In other words, the national accounts also reinforce the case for improving the quality of the fiscal spending adjustment ahead.

Figure 2: Consumption and investment
![](images/03fbd09ef409cadbf8cb140bd1872ebe60eb6329655e0b3c5b09adf6f81435a3.jpg)
Source: INDEC and J.P. Morgan

Net exports have shown a very good performance, helping to ameliorate the effect of the demand compression on overall GDP. Real exports gained for a third consecutive quarter, up by 52%ar helped by the normalization of agriculture production and energy. On the other side, imports crashed by 40%ar in the first quarter, explained by the domestic demand collapse. Note that real imports ran 22%,sa below the peak logged in the second quarter of last year.

GDP

In all, the sequential contraction reported for the first quarter proved less onerous than expected. Ahead, our base case is for activity growth to stabilize in the second quarter, with sequential GDP turning positive (+1.0%q/q, saar). The incoming high frequency data have proved a mixed bag. The April economic indicator will help us to fine-tune the projection for the second quarter of the year. Following the 1Q national accounts report, the 2024 annual GDP growth is finetuned to -3.4%y/y (from -3.6%, previously).

## Accrued CAB surprised to the downside

The 1Q24 current account posted a US\$240mn surplus, implying a strong reversal from the US\$5.7bn shortfall reported in same quarter last year. The bulk of the improvement was explained by the trade balance recovery, which saw a US\$5.4bn reversal into a US\$5bn surplus (accrued basis). This was driven by an increase in exports (+US\$1.5bn on the quarter) amid the normalization of agri conditions but more so by a decline in imports (-US\$3.8bn) amid the domestic demand contraction, as noted above. Meanwhile, the services deficit narrowed by US\$1.2bn, mainly explained by lower tourism outflows on the back of December’s devaluation, which was in part offset by a US\$0.6bn widening in the income shortfall on higher interest payments, to both the market and the IMF.

As we have been highlighting in prior notes, it is worth noting the gap between the accrued and the cash basis current account, reflecting the underlying distortions arising from persistent capital controls. Indeed, while the cash-basis current account posted a sizeable US\$5.3bn surplus, the accrued one only reported US\$240mn. Such a wide US\$5.1bn quarterly gap was explained by higher accrued imports (goods + services) for US\$10.2bn, the flipside of new importers’ commercial debt accumulation on the quarter amid restrictions on import payments (the current framework allows for payment of imports in four monthly installments). On the other side, this was partially offset by higher exports reported on an accrued basis for US\$5bn, as the cash basis only reports 80% of the export inflows that are supplied to the official FX market (the other 20% go through the parallel FX).

We revise lower our 2024 current account (accrued basis) forecast to US\$4bn (0.7% of our estimated GDP). While we expect the cash-basis current account surplus to print at a high US\$12bn on the year, the capital controls are to drive the accrued current account surplus much lower, to US\$4bn in our estimates. This would still imply a strong improvement from the US\$21bn current account deficit (3.2% of GDP) recorded last year. Such improvement is mainly predicated on the recovery in the trade balance amid a normalization of exports and a contraction in imports. We now wait for the release of the cash-basis current account data for May to finetune our reserves accumulation forecast if need be. As we highlighted repeatedly, advancing towards the release of capital controls in 3Q should only secure and accelerate the ability to replenish reserves in the medium term.

Figure 3: Accrued vs. cash basis current account
![](images/33611b2b87679272d9730888b4894af1182b6989739071aff3e09ff57cdd1a0d.jpg)
Source: INDEC, BCRA and J.P. Morgan

## Andeans

Peru: The last 12-m headline (primary) deficit widened to 3.9% (2.2%) of GDP in May

• On a sequential basis, the annualized last 3-month primary deficit ran at 1.2% of GDP

The 2024 fiscal deficit is now projected at 2.8% of GDP

## Peru: A wider fiscal deficit

The fiscal situation in Peru has deteriorated more than we expected in the first half of the year. In fact, the headline fiscal deficit measured on a 12-month basis increased from 2.8% to 3.9% of GDP between December 2023 and May 2024, mainly due to a reduction in current revenues (Figure 1). Revenues were affected by the lower regularization of income tax, as well as the postponement of the deadline for annual tax returns and regularization for individuals and SMEs from March-April to May-June 2024. To a lesser extent, non-tax revenues also contributed to the revenues decline, notably the items of royalties and oil and gas. On the spending side, the increase in non-financial expenditures was mainly due to higher gross capital formation at the three levels of government and remunerations, due to the salary increase granted to both the education and health sectors as well as to the various labor regimes in the public sector.

Figure 1: Peru central government revenues and expenditures % of GDP, last 12 months
![](images/148ca039ddd00ce503fca7ebc9efd2d44af10ccee1e70edd9a0d821524172dea.jpg)

The sequential analysis shows negative momentum for both real revenues and expenditures. When adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan’s adjustment), real revenues dropped by 19.9%3m/3m, saar in May. On its side, non-financial spending recorded a more moderate 5.9%3m/3m, saar contraction. In all, the last 3-month annualized primary deficit is now running at 1.2% of GDP, while the effective primary deficit for the past 12 months as of May escalated to 2.2% of GDP.

Following the May fiscal report, the annual headline fiscal deficit is now projected at 2.8% for this year, in line with the maximum limit established by the text approved in the first vote by Congress on the legislative powers project in fiscal balance matters. The upward revision for 2024 is mainly explained by the slower progress recorded in revenue collection until May, the downward revision of the primary result of state-owned enterprises, and the higher expenses in gross capital formation. The annual projection also includes a reduction in non-financial expenses as a percentage of GDP, mainly in current expenses, due to the dissipation of extraordinary expenses incurred in temporary programs and a reduction in capex.

Finally, when controlling for the impact of cyclical, temporary, and extraordinary components that affect the economy, the structural primary deficit is estimated to converge to 1.4% in 2024, widening when compared to 2023’s 0.9% of GDP. The widening reflects the rebound in capex. For 2025, a reduction of the structural fiscal deficit is expected, in line with higher structural revenues and public spending control.

## United Kingdom

Latest polls indicate Labour is heading for a landslide election victory next week

Little fiscal detail expected until the next Budget, likely later this Autumn

• No near-term implications for the BoE

• 1Q GDP revised up to 0.7%q/q, prompted by private consumption

The polls send a strong message that Labour will not just win next week’s general election, but will see a landslide victory. The national polls point to a lead for Labour of around 20 points (Figure 1). More sophisticated micro-based MRP data help to map vote shares into seats, and suggest Labour could gain a seat majority of over 200 (Table 1).

Figure 1: UK polls tracker for next general election

This would be a larger margin of victory than Tony Blair’s Labour Party managed in 1997, and potentially the largest seen since WWII (Table 2). Even allowing for a significant error around this estimate, Labour would still have what would be regarded as a solid majority. Boris Johnson’s Conservative Party achieved a majority of 81 in 2019, and that was considered a large majority at the times. A large majority tends to limit the influence from the fringes of the political party forming the government and hands the party significant power.

Some have asked whether this could produce a leftward shift in the Labour party over time, closer to its traditional ideological roots. This is a possibility, but Labour is only in its current position because it has managed to attract many floating voters from the Conservatives. The party leadership will likely want to remain close to the centre to retain these voters beyond this forthcoming parliament. A strong majority gives the party leadership authority to defend the stable and more centrist stance it is seeking a mandate for. By contrast, if the majority were weak there would be a greater chance of a change in leadership during the next parliament that shifts the party leftward.

Little is known about the details of Labour’s approach to fiscal policy, and this is unlikely to change immediately following the election result. The party is communicating that there will be no major tax rises, no significant spending cuts and no slippage from the current fiscal rules. But absent good news on growth it will be difficult to achieve all three.

More will become clear at the next Budget. The OBR requires 10 weeks notice to prepare its forecasts. More detail could hence come in September, but Labour may choose to wait until after its annual party conference late that month to hold the budget (as well as an overdue spending review setting out 2024/25 departmental allocations). Hence October or November might be more realistic.

Our expectation is that Labour will begin by broadly sticking to the existing overall projection that shows a structural fiscal tightening of 0.6% next year. Within that, the composition is likely to tilt more towards tax increases rather than spending restraint, as shown in the current plans. But we expect there will be restraint for non-protected departments. Through time, we anticipate the party will first seek to maximise leeway around its fiscal rules rather than significant tax increases. An initially unchanged fiscal path would imply there are no real implications for the BoE, where we expect an August cut to be followed up by another in November.

An upward revision to consumption from 0.2% to 0.4% in 1Q helped to produce an upgrade to overall GDP from 0.6% to 0.7%. The revision came despite downgrades to the pace of government consumption and business investment. The near 3% annualised pace for GDP in 1Q likely overshoots where the trend is but yet is more in line with what would have been expected given the strong pace of real household income growth. We expect growth to moderate slightly but remain strong at 2% in 2Q, led again by income strength. The latest PMI hints at some growth slowdown into 3Q.

## Sweden and Norway

● Riksbank guides towards 2-3 cuts in 2H24...

• ...contrasting with Norges Bank guiding only for 1Q25

• We expect only a partial reversal of Swiftflation in June

As widely expected, the Riksbank unanimously kept the policy rate on hold this week. The Board did, however, surprise with a dovish rate path. Instead of guiding for two cuts in the second half of the year (as in March and as widely expected), the policy rate was projected to be cut 2-3 times. Moreover, a high probability of a cut in August was shown compared to a coin toss at the March MPR.

Ahead of the August meeting there are two inflation prints. The Riksbank’s forecast is one-tenth above ours for both June and July (more below). Such a deviation is likely too small to persuade the Riksbank to not cut in August. Looking at other data (GDP, U-rate, KIX), our forecasts are also a little bit more hawkish, but again, probably too small to change course. As a consequence, we change our forecast from a next cut in September to August. We do not change our forecast beyond September, however, as the forward guidance aligns with our long-held call of a total of three cuts in 2H24.

The most important release in the coming weeks will be the June inflation report where the big question is how much of the Swiftflation impact from May will be reversed (Figure 1). In our view, a full reversal is unlikely already in June. This was the lesson last year when Beyonce visited. We expect core prices to rise 0.3%m/m (nsa, 2.6%oya ), roughly in line with the usual pre-pandemic seasonality in June. Our forecast is 0.1%-pt above the Riksbank. In contrast, headline CPIF inflation should fall well below 2% (and stay below for the rest of 2024), owing to a sharp fall in electricity prices and base effects.

Figure 1: Swiftflation contribution to monthly CPI change
![](images/e517fd4d439dba81b28063327674c243dcee055f6ad44f63d72e1af61f6d4856.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

On the activity side, we expect the (volatile) monthly GDP indicator to rebound 0.5%m/m after a sharp 0.7%m/m contraction in April. An upward revision to the April reading also seems likely. For the June PMIs, we expect further improvement—in accordance with the June NIER survey. The manufacturing index should rise to around its long-run average, consistent with various leading indicators such as metal prices, order-to-inventory ratios, and the Asian manufacturing cycle. The services PMI should move above the 50 threshold. Despite an expected rebound in the above-mentioned indicators we still look for a small GDP contraction this quarter of 0.5%q/q, saar. This reflects a weak start to the quarter and some reversal of the substantial inventory building in 1Q.

## Norges Bank signals no cut in 2024

As universally expected, Norges Bank kept the policy rate on hold at 4.5% at the June meeting. The Committee did surprise, though, with its forward guidance, expecting only a first cut in 1Q25 (vs. September at the last MPR). The rate path did not even show a risk of a cut in 2H24, but instead a (very) small risk of a hike. Although our calculation of the technical rate path had actually indicated a similar outcome, we had assumed that the so-called judgment component would pull down the rate path in 2H24, not up (Figure 2). Despite the hawkish outcome we do not change our call for a cut in December.

Figure 2: Decomposition of Norges Bank's rate path, June 2024
![](images/302dff4d1ff270109085a0f2e69b04b6f48c62a615c2b25908be42db834f22bf.jpg)

We expect core prices to rise 0.4%m/m, nsa, in June (3.6%oya), in line with Norges Bank’s forecast. The forecast is below the typical seasonal pattern in June as we assume both food and services prices will be soft after strong outcomes in recent months. From July, we expect core inflation to undershoot Norges Bank’s forecast—as has been the case for almost all months over the past year. The Bank thus appears to be expecting a very strong outcome for food prices. Historically, food prices have indeed been very strong in July given the semi-annual negotiations between suppliers, but this practice was changed last year. An expected large undershooting of core inflation versus the Bank’s forecast is one of the key reasons why we think Norges Bank will cut in December, ahead of its guidance for 1Q25.

## Emerging Europe

• Czech Republic: CNB surprises with deeper than expected rate cut

• Türkiye: CBRT on hold at 50% with hawkish bias as expected

• Poland: Inflation stays close to target

## Czech Republic: CNB surprises

The CNB surprised on the dovish side, cutting the 2-week repo rate by 50bps for a fourth consecutive time, to 4.75%, delaying the deceleration in the easing pace expected by us and consensus (to 25bp steps). Though not a base case, the decision to cut 50bps cannot be said to be a huge surprise as several board members mentioned in public that it would be a discussion between a 25bp or a 50bp cut.

After a sequence of 50bp cuts wrapped in increasingly hawkish rhetoric, the CNB needed to deliver some corroboration of that hawkishness. The first concrete hawkish signal is the vote split: the decision to cut 50bps was supported by five board members, whereas two would prefer slowing the pace to 25bp moves. This compares to a straight 7-0 (in favour of 50bps) in the last meeting, and with the minutes revealing that two members had considered even deeper cuts of 75bps.

Second, in addition to the comment that “the interest rate reduction process can be paused or terminated at any time at levels that are still restrictive if inflation – especially its core component – does not develop in line with the forecast”, there is now specific commentary by Governor Michl that the next move is “more likely to be 25bps or a pause” (cited by Bloomberg). In our view the communication mix sets a high bar for the easing cycle to continue in 50bp moves and hence signals quite forcefully a reduction in the pace from August (there is no meeting in July). We expect the CNB to slowdown the easing pace to 25bp/meeting from August, continuing at that pace until reaching 4%, which we maintain as our year-end forecast (Figure 1).

Figure 1: CNB interest rate outlook
![](images/210322baf5f9900048664ab3fd2a1ebdf7259524eb93a3b847023b16cf280965.jpg)

## Türkiye: CBRT on hold at 50%

The CBRT kept the policy rate on hold at 50%, in line with our expectation and market consensus. The CBRT kept its hawkish bias by reiterating that it is ready to tighten in case significant deterioration in inflation is foreseen. It noted that the decline in the underlying trend of monthly inflation registered a temporary pause in May, and that recent indicators confirm that domestic demand growth is slowing, although still at inflationary levels. We maintain our end-2024 policy rate forecast of 45% as we expect the CBRT to cut its policy rate by 250bp each in November and December.

Headline inflation is likely to fall to 2.1% m/m (2.3% m/m sa) in June, down from 3.4% (3.4% m/m) in May. Over-yearago headline inflation is set to register its first fall since June 2023. Headline inflation is expected to fall towards 72.3% oya in June, from 75.4% in May. Electricity and natural gas price hikes are expected in July and October and we assume these will be in line with inflation targets. We expect a sharp fall towards 50% oya in August on the back of favorable base effects and a slowdown in the monthly inflation momentum. Our end 2024 inflation forecast is at 43.5% as fiscal policy is likely to be more supportive of disinflation through reduced government spending, direct tax measures, and no minimum wage hike in 2H24 (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Turkey CPI inflation forecasts
![](images/e5837e5183e0be9ad351593ea3f8f493aa5ba128e9ea95148bbc918ffbcd1203.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, TurkStat

The CBRT is about to eliminate its FX swap book with local banks, and hinted at new tools for sterilization of excess lira liquidity. The average deposit rate up to 3 months fell to 58.4% as of 21 June, down from 68.2% on 10 May (Figure 3) as the Turkish banks are seeing downside pressure on their profitability due to muted lira credit growth. The CBRT hinted further sterilization measures to mop up excess lira liquidity in case of unanticipated developments in credit and deposit markets. The CBRT unwound FX swaps with local banks sharply in 2Q24, and withdraw excess lira liquidity through this channel in 2Q24 in addition to RRR hikes. The CBRT reduced its FX swap stock from \$58.5bn on April 1<sup>st</sup> to only \$5bn as of 26 June. The CBRT is likely to eliminate its FX swap book with local banks within weeks. That\`s why CBRT

Source: GUS

is likely to introduce new sterilization measure in the nearterm.

![](images/265a8c37f49e9677785eb8a92c977558815bbeb48bd8cacc76e2500110e06507.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, CBRT

## Poland: Inflation stays close to target

According to the preliminary release, inflation remained close to the NBP’s 2.5%oya target for a fifth consecutive month, rising marginally to 2.6%oya (from 2.5%), in line with market expectations but above our forecast (Consensus: 2.6%oya, JPM: 2.3%). Looking at the scarce details available for now (Table 1), we see that food prices moved faster than the recent trend, increasing 0.7%m/m, which is quite stronger than we expected (-0.2%m/m). In the opposite direction energy prices remain predictably subdued. Fuel prices dropped 2.8%m/m, a bit more (but not that far off) the indication of the weekly surveys (-2.3%m/m), while “other energy” fell 0.1% on the month, as expected.

Table 1: Poland CPI details<sup>1</sup>

1 June figures are preliminary 2 June core CPI is J.P. Morgan estimate

Core CPI is not published with the flash release, but if we mechanically extract the contributions of non-core items, we are left with a residual which suggests core prices were up 0.15%m/m, slightly above our forecast (0.08%m/m). If confirmed, it implies core CPI falling to 3.7%oya in May, from 3.8%, a bit above our forecast (3.6%). This leaves the average 2Q24 core inflation at 3.9%oya, sharply below the NBP’s 4.4% projection. In seasonally-adjusted terms, we estimate that core CPI was up 0.34%m/m (sa), which is around 4.1% in annualized terms. However, given the preliminary nature of the release as well as the lack of decimal precision there are decent error bands around this estimate, anything between 3% and 5% is a reasonable estimate until we get complete data. At face value though, the June print appears comparable to the range of the past months even if marginally higher: on a

3-month basis, core momentum is running around 3.5%, the upper limit of the NBP’s target tolerance band (2.5%oya +/- 15-pt). The final release will provide more colour, in particular if the weak core goods CPI momentum was sustained in June despite the interruptions to the Zloty’s appreciation trend.

July CPI will be impacted by upward adjustments to energy tariffs resulting from the removal of some of the price caps introduced by the previous government at the time of Europe’s energy price spike. According to statements by energy regulator URE yesterday, electricity tariffs will be increased by 32% (resulting from increases in both the electricity tariff and in the distribution component), but the final impact is likely to be somewhat lower as the capacity fee becomes zero till year-end, and at the same time there are 3.5 million lower-income households who will benefit from an offsetting energy voucher, the treatment of which by the statistical office is not clear. Separately, gas prices will go up 20% according to the regulator, including also large adjustments in the transmission fees, which we expected to be more benign. These two adjustments will likely add something like 1.7%-pts to headline inflation, which as a result will shift from the current levels to the 4.5-5% range in 2H24.

## Poland:

<table><tr><td>Mon PMI</td><td colspan="5"></td></tr><tr><td>Jul 1</td><td>Index</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>9:00am</td><td>PMI, Manufac-</td><td>Mar 48.0</td><td>Apr 45.9</td><td>May 45.0</td><td>Jun 44.5</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>turing</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Tue</td><td>NBP rate decision</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jul 2</td><td>On hold at 5.75%</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr></table>

We expect the NBP to keep rates unchanged at 5.75% next week. Data has come on the dovish side, with inflation in particular surprising significantly on the downside, and fluctuating at close-to-target levels. Nonetheless, a deep hawkish bias prevails in the MPC , and the preference remains to be firmly on hold. Hence, we expect the message to focus on risks stemming from regulated price increases and strong wage growth.

Source: NBP, Local sources, J.P. Morgan Forecasts

## Romania:

<table><tr><td>Thu</td><td>NBR rate decision</td></tr><tr><td>Jul 4</td><td>25bps cut to 6.75%</td></tr></table>

Our view remains that core inflation momentum is too elevated (around 8.5% and 7% 3m saar for core goods and services, respectively) to allow rate cuts. But the large recent downside CPI surprises would allow the dovish-leaning NBR Board to start the easing cycle this week with a 25bp rate cut to 6.75%. Yet, the elevated core inflation is likely to limit how much NBR can cut – we see a shallow cycle of maximum two rate cuts this year to 6.50%.

Headline CPI is likely to fall to 2.1% m/m in June (2.3% m/m sa), down from 3.4% m/m in May thanks to a slowdown in domestic demand. Headline over-year-ago inflation is set to fall from 75.4% oya in May to 72.3% in June thanks to favourable base effects. Headline inflation is likely to end 2024 at 43.5% and end 2025 at 25.2% as fiscal policy is likely to be more supportive of disinflation in 2H24 and 2025.

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia’s monthly CPI was stronger than expected in May, rising 4%oya nsa

• We still expect the RBA to remain on hold over 2024, but tightening risks are rising

The RBA minutes should maintain a hawkish tone, noting the Board discussed hiking

• We expect Australian retail sales to increase 0.2%m/m sa, another soft outcome

Australia's May CPI indicator was stronger than we and consensus had expected at 4.0%oya nsa (JPM and consensus 3.8%oya, Figure 1). The miss is a little less than it appears on face value in that the delta vs the %m/m was closer to 0.1%-pt (-0.1%m/m, nsa), though we had argued that, even on an in-line result, the optics of a rising %oya rate would see the market squeeze a bit more RBA hike probability into August. After today’s data, that now stands at around a 40% prospect of a hike.

Figure 1: Australia monthly CPI indicator
![](images/285e7edb8d489d68aeaf5a36d1fd6fd93ff2fe498e741517f19875b86f859915.jpg)

The RBA's standing forecasts in the SoMP are for 1%q/q nsa and 0.8%q/q nsa on headline and trimmed mean inflation, respectively, for 2Q. We see around one-tenth of upside risk on trimmed mean, though headline still looks broadly okay to us (we have 1.0%q/q on headline, up from 0.9%q/q before this week's data), given the reconciliation from monthly to quarterly has some idiosyncracies; moreover, the details showed surprises coming from food prices.

In our view, the RBA has a relatively high bar to hike again and we expect the cash rate to stay on hold this year as growth has slowed, policy is restrictive and the mix of the inflation appears consistent with achievement of the target next year. Though of course the full 2Q report will be important, and a simple rolling 3m calculation using 2022/23 placeholder values for the June monthly puts the 2Q tracking above 1%q/q nsa. It is likely market participants continue to perceive upside risks to the RBA's forecasts on that basis, which is not likely to be corrected until the quarterly result is delivered in the back half of July.

Recall the April result had been boosted in part by supply difficulties in a relatively narrow subset of the food basket, and we had expected some reversal in May. That wasn't delivered, due to a broader pick-up, and the food/non-alcoholic beverages group had an almost identical gain to April, at +0.5%m/m nsa (Figure 2).

![](images/7ac7b62839b895c38f1ef9fef5d7810dc19480e69cde0189672aa72eb60300b9.jpg)

Services prices were flat overall, with a significant decline in the recreation and culture group (-1.3%m/m, nsa) which was expected on the basis of seasonally weaker pricing for holiday travel and accommodation. Education and health didn't move, which is normal given the monthly collection/updating profile, while finance/insurance had another strong gain (0.7%m/m, nsa) due to the insurance category. Insurance prices continue to be the strongest performer in the CPI basket reflecting structural factors and lags to headline inflation, though we think regulatory pricing constraints are likely to bind relatively soon from here.

Figure 3: Australia housing related inflation
![](images/8e1d16e785a5d8df0be34150ac0530cf44fe9245fccb72838a70881286549d25.jpg)

In the housing group, electricity prices firmed quite a lot (1.4%m/m, nsa) as subsidy support waned, though that is due to be refreshed in July with extension of Commonwealth support, and lower underlying wholesale prices also suggest a downward bias. This is also worth keeping in mind given the likelihood of downward revisions to the RBA's 2H24 inflation forecasts, which had not incorporated the extension of subsidies announced in the Budget. Rents, however, accelerated to 0.8%m/m nsa, while still slipping a tenth in annual terms to 7.4%oya nsa.

## RBA minutes to maintain the hawkish tone

We expect the minutes from the RBA’s June policy decision will preserve many of the themes from prior meeting and retain the guidance that Officials are not “ruling anything in or out” with respect to future changes to the cash rate. In the press conference following the June meeting, Governor Bullock noted the Board only discussed the merits of hiking vs leaving rates unchanged, and we expect the minutes to provide a more detailed assessment of these discussions. The minutes obviously wont incorporate the stronger than forecast May CPI indicator, but it’s worth noting that the recent inflation data have generally landed to the high side of expectations meaning it’s likely the inflation narrative also skews hawkish.

## Data releases and forecasts

Week of Jul 1 - 5

<table><tr><td>Tue Retail sales</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jul 2</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>11:30am %m/m, sa</td><td></td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.4 0.1</td><td>0.2</td></tr></table>

Retail sales rose just 0.1%m/m, sa in April, a soft outcome in the context of the prior month’s outright decline (-0.4%m/m). We forecast another modest increase in the coming week’s data (+0.2%m/m) with increased speculation of further RBA rate hikes likely weighing on consumer sentiment and spending behavior. With that in mind, we retain the view that the annual pace of household consumption has likely troughed with rising real wage growth and personal income tax cuts to support consumption in 2H24.

<table><tr><td>Wed</td><td>Building approvals</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Jul 3</td><td></td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>11:30am</td><td>%m/m, sa</td><td>-0.5</td><td>2.7</td><td>-0.3</td><td>2</td></tr></table>

We think next Australian building approvals will increase 2%m/m sa in the May data. Approvals declined modestly in April, led by falls in approvals for private sector detached dwellings. Since the pandemic, approvals have tended to fall in April and rebound strongly in May, implying there may be some residual seasonality in the series. We thus expect a bounce back in May. More broadly, we think approvals have troughed this cycle, and should trend upwards on the back of higher population growth and public sector support.

<table><tr><td>Thu</td><td>Trade balance</td></tr><tr><td>Jul 4</td><td>Feb</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td></tr><tr><td>11:30am</td><td>A$bn, sa</td><td>6.7</td><td>4.8 6.5</td><td>6</td></tr></table>

We anticipate the trade balance on goods will print at A\$6bn, sa in the May release, a deterioration of around A\$0.5bn relative to April. The balance was particularly volatile over Feb-Apr, which mainly reflected a large rise and subsequent fall in imports of processed industrial supplies. In level terms, this series is back close to the 2H23 average, and we don’t expect any further outsized impacts on the aggregate balance on goods. The RBA’s index of commodity prices continued to trend lower over May, and we think further drags from commodity exports will drive the balance lower in the latest print.

## Greater China

• China: PBOC governor defensive while claiming monetary policy is expansionary

• Industrial profits growth narrowed to 0.7%oya in May

• Hong Kong: May merchandise exports rose 14.8%oya

Taiwan: May industrial activity jumped on tech sector strength

• Next week: China PMIs, Hong Kong retail sales, Taiwan PMI, inflation

PBOC Governor Pan delivered a speech at Lujiazui Forum on June 19, titled “Current monetary policy stance in China and future evolution of monetary policy framework”. Governor Pan used “supportive monetary policy” (literal meaning is expansionary) to replace the conventional wording “prudential monetary policy”. In our view, recent monetary policy operations are inconsistent with the “supportive” characterization. Governor Pan was defensive on China’s monetary policy stance, and emphasized that the PBOC has taken measures to address the “idle credit” phenomenon, to improve the efficiency of monetary policy transmission.

The discrepancy between the statement and PBOC operational policy reflects the need to balance multiple objectives. Governor Pan elaborated that decisions should balance three trade-offs: 1) near-term versus long-term objectives (countercyclical and through-the-cycle policy considerations); 2) growth versus risk prevention; and 3) internal versus external factors. These multiple objectives lead to difficulties in monetary policy decisions. We expect the PBOC will continue to cut policy rates and RRR for the rest of the year, but the timing might be postponed and the magnitude of rate cuts constrained. We expect one 10bp rate cut and another 25bp RRR cut, with TSF growth at 8.5% by end-2024 (vs. 9.5% in 2023).

Governor Pan’s elaboration on the future evolution of the monetary policy framework provided new insights and raised new questions. Pan suggested that the importance of quantitative measures will decline and price-based measures will be more important. Interest rate policy will shift to focus on the 7-day reverse repo rate as the only key policy rate, which means the importance of the 1-year MLF rate will decline going forward. And the PBOC will enrich its monetary policy toolbox by trading Treasury Bonds in the open market, but it does not mean additional policy stimulus or adoption of China’s QE. The PBOC has expanded the usage of structural monetary policy instruments, which are supplementary to conventional monetary policy instruments, along with exit mechanisms when the policy purpose is achieved. The PBOC will also try to improve policy transparency, by establishing regular policy communications and strengthening expectations management.

## Industrial profits growth narrowed

China’s total industrial profits grew 3.4%oya in January-May, following 4.3%oya growth in Jan-Apr (Figure 1). The midsingle-digit growth rate came off a low base, as industrial profits fell 18.8%oya in Jan-May 2023. For the month of May, the NBS estimated that industrial profits rose 0.7%oya. Meanwhile, sales revenue grew 2.9%oya in Jan-May 2024, and cost of sales increased by 3.0%oya. Profit margin rose to 5.19, compared with 5.00% in Jan-Apr. By ownership type, profits of foreign enterprises and private enterprises continued to outperform, growing 12.6%oya and 7.6%oya, respectively.

Figure 1: China industrial enterprises' profits and sales revenue
![](images/8d523d6ab006984b486e89ef734461f1e7e0c7a2b90b3e7f315faa0a42723cf0.jpg)
Source: NBS, J.P. Morgan

The pace of industrial enterprise profits growth looks set to moderate further. Domestically, the housing market drag continues. The authorities announced a new round of housing policy relaxation and created a new re-lending facility of 300bn yuan in mid-May. But the impact of that has not materialized in the data yet. Weakness in housing has depressed demand for construction-related sectors, and negative wealth effects from declining housing prices have constrained demand more broadly. On the external front, the new lifting forces of the economy (NEV, solar penal, battery) are facing intensifying challenges from the Western world. Following the Biden administration’s announcement of tariff increases on selected Chinese goods, the European Commission on June 12 announced the provisional conclusion of its investigation into Chinese EV exports. In the absence of agreement between the two sides by July 4, Chinese electric cars will face 27.4% to 48.1% of total tariff.

## Hong Kong: May exports rose 14.8%oya

Hong Kong’s May merchandise exports came in line with market expectations, rising 14.8%oya to HK\$375.9bn, following 11.9%oya growth in April (Figure 2). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that the city’s exports rose 0.2%m/m sa in May, after rising 3.8%m/m sa in April. Meanwhile, imports rose 9.6%oya to HK\$388.1bn. Seasonally adjusted, we estimate the city’s imports rose 1.3%m/m sa in May, reversing the 1.6%m/m sa decline in April. Monthly merchandise trade activities realized a deficit of HK\$12.1bn.

Figure 2: Hong Kong merchandise trade
![](images/46af114da294a0b5c97a510f0259691bf2d461cc1ce5884f798100e1f7bebd4e.jpg)
Source: HK C&SD, J.P. Morgan

HK’s FX reserves edged up \$0.8bn in May, staying at an abundant level. Hong Kong’s FX reserves have fallen from the peak level of US\$449bn in November 2021. The official FX reserves of HK were recorded at US\$417.2bn in May, up from US\$416.4bn in April. Meanwhile, 1Q24 Current Account recorded a surplus of HK\$101.0bn, which is about 13.1% of the 1Q24 nominal GDP. In further details, the goods deficit narrowed to HK\$6.7bn, compared with a HK\$7.7bn deficit in 4Q23. Goods credit was HK\$1,142bn in 1Q24, while goods debit was HK\$1,149bn. Service surplus widened to HK\$50.8bn in 1Q24 vs. HK\$27.8bn in 4Q23, with service credit at HK\$227.2bn and service debit at HK\$176.4bn.

## Taiwan: May industrial activity jumped

Taiwan’s May IP report came in significantly above expectations. IP rose 16.06%oya in May, following 14.48% growth in April. Seasonally-adjusted, IP jumped 6.4% m/m sa in May, adding to the gain of 2.0% m/m in April, with strong sequential trend growth at 15.1% 3m/3m saar. The strong May IP report was led by tech-related IP (Figure 3), which jumped 11.3% m/m sa (or growing 31.9% 3m/3m saar). Non-tech IP rose 1.7% m/m sa (or growing 1.2% 3m/3m saar). Following general weakness since early 2022, machinery and equipment manufacturing IP has ticked up lately, rising 9.2% 3m/3m saar in May, consistent with a constructive outlook on the global capex trend. The significant upturn in May industrial activity came on the back of constructive signals on near-term external demand conditions (as suggested by the upside surprise in the April and May export orders reports), hinting at solid export growth going into June/ July.

Consistent upward momentum in the external sector seems to be feeding to the domestic front. The unemployment rate edged down to 3.35% sa in May, compared to 3.39% in April. Total employment appears to have picked up some momentum lately, rising 0.3% m/m sa in May, with increases across industrial and service sector employment. A gradual upturn in employment growth (and hence household income), if sustained, could help to support domestic demand growth going into 2H24. On a similarly positive note, retail sales rose 1.8% m/m sa in May (or growing at solid pace of 6.9% 3m/3m saar).

Figure 3: Taiwan tech and nontech manufacturing IP (level) Index, 2011=100, sa, 3mma
![](images/caf9b8ec75dd579ac7a46e1a0601fe27852a25e3afdee5fe2e3e28cdcdf30e54.jpg)

## Review of past week’s data

Industrial production (24 Jun)

The long-form nomenclature for references to China; Hong Kong; and Taiwan within this research material is Mainland China; Hong Kong SAR (China); and Taiwan (China).

## Korea

• Industrial production fell in May yet tech sector improved

• Domestic demand remained soft in May

• In all, the monthly data confirms the expected slowdown in 2Q growth

This week’s monthly activity data confirms the expected slowdown in 2Q real GDP growth. Industrial production fell in May, but the April-May level is still higher than the 1Q average, suggesting a modest recovery in quarterly IP growth in 2Q. Domestic demand and non-manufacturing activities were also disappointing, but not to the extent to change the quarterly growth forecast. If we aggregate IP and non-manufacturing activities, the monthly GDP tracker (non-farm allindustry output) fell 0.7%m/m, sa after a 1.2% gain in April, and the three-month trend growth turned to -3.2% 3m/3m, saar. That said, we still expect 2Q GDP growth to be at 0.5%q/q, saar, as we have already incorporated a material slowdown in our 2Q forecast after the strong 1Q print (5.3%q/q, saar) (Figure 1). As for the implications for the July MPC meeting, we believe the data point may not significantly affect the members’ outlook and BoK’s macro forecast given the monthly volatility in the data.

Figure 1: Real GDP and non-farm all-industry output
![](images/495dedb4f9db12f5135ff0e0f380870f1c4e099c8366864e3128e797b559463a.jpg)

## IP fell in May with monthly volatility

Industrial production fell 1.2%m/m, sa in May, but the monthly decline was after a strong and upwardly revised April production index. In the three-month trend, IP contracted 2.2%3m/3m, saar in May, yet the April-May average level was 2.5% ar, higher than the 1Q average. We maintain our expectation for a modest IP recovery in 2Q (3-4% annualized quarterly growth) after a brief pause in 1Q.

In the sector details, tech production rose modestly by 1.3%m/m, sa after falling for two months, while non-tech production fell 2.1% after a 4.7% gain in April. The tech production gain was led by a 1.8%m/m, sa growth in semiconductor production, amid a strong 18.8% rebound in semiconductor shipments. Tech inventory fell 3.9% in May, and the inventory-to-shipment ratio fell back in the tech sector as an upbeat detail. We continue to expect that tech production will resume trend growth again, after a slowdown from 76.6%3m/3m, saar in October 2023 to -8.9% in May. The non-tech sector production broadly fell in May, as a partial payback after April’s gain. Non-tech production’s trend growth remained positive at 1.2%3m/3m, saar (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Manufacturing production %3m/3m, saar, both axes
![](images/700cff07fbb563c51882a67f0e85bf75f56dc3cfe9bd1ff86c94b58db91489a0.jpg)

## Domestic demand was soft in May

Retail sales fell by 0.2%m/m, sa, in May, following a 0.8% drop in April. It was the third monthly fall in the past four months, with a sharp 3.2% drop in February and a partial recovery in March. Auto sales rebounded partially by 3.0%m/ m, from a 12.6% drop in April, after being bumpy this year around the timing of new EV subsidies in February-March. The sequential growth of overall retail sales marked a 5.2% 3m/3m, contraction in May, the third consecutive month in negative territory, though narrower than 7.5% a month ago. The retail goods sector has been subdued so far this year, but spending on services likely provided an offset and led to robust growth in overall private consumption in the GDP report (2.9%q/q, saar, in 1Q24). That said, services output recently softened to some extent, and its growth turned to a slight negative at -2.1%3m/3m, saar, in May after nine months in positive territory, suggesting a near-term moderation in the domestic-oriented sector in 2Q24 (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Private consumption and monthly indicators
![](images/678f23ec0d7ecbc45dbc536e5af5e79eb8424ed4cfc0477778df20cca275b20c.jpg)

Meanwhile, construction investment fell 4.6%m/m, sa, in May, after falling 10.2% in March and recovering partially by 4.2% in April. In sequential terms, construction investment contracted 21.9%3m/3m, saar, in May, after decelerating sharply from +21.1% in March to 1.0% in April. This aligns with our view that the construction sector should experience a correction throughout this year, with the gradual completion of existing projects and a decline in new projects in the high interest rate environment over the past year.
