## ASEAN

ASEAN’s US\$ liquidity is broadly ample based on IMF’s ARA metric but with wide intra-regional variation

• Reserve adequacy levels differ by economic/BOP structures and some caveats apply

• Dollarization behavior on negative/narrow rate differentials add more pressure on reserves

Using the IMF’s comprehensive ARA methodology, we find that US\$ liquidity is broadly ample in ASEAN, but with wide variation between countries. Most of the decrease in liquidity buffers since 2021 is the result of reserves drawdown instead of worsening fundamentals. Reserve adequacy (RA) levels differ by economic/BOP structures and additional caveats apply when it comes to the role of short-term external debt. Going forward, if negative/narrow rate differentials with the US persists, the region’s reserves could face added pressure from dollarization behavior.

## Casting a wider net on reserve assessment

When it comes to assessing the adequacy of a central bank’s international reserves, investors tend to adopt heuristics in the form of coverage ratios (e.g., imports, short-term external debt). While easily computed and understood, this approach does not fully capture the different external risks and vulnerabilities of an economy, making cross-country comparisons less effective. For example, a country with a superior import coverage ratio does not necessarily have more adequate reserves if it is more susceptible to other types of FX outflows (e.g., portfolio, debt repayment).

To better facilitate “apples-to-apples” comparison, we apply the IMF’s Assessing Reserve Adequacy (ARA) methodology. This calculates a country’s required level of reserves (RA) to address external shocks, based on the strength of inflows (i.e., exports) and the vulnerability to outflows (e.g., short-term external debt, foreign portfolio liability stock, and domestic money supply). The ratio between domestic US\$ liquidity (sum of central bank net reserves and excess US\$ liquidity in the domestic banking system) and IMF’s RA is then interpreted as a country’s reserves buffer.

## Wide variation in US\$ liquidity buffers

Based on IMF’s guidance, a reserves/ARA metric ratio of 1.0-1.5x is considered adequate. Using this standard, our analysis shows that reserves across ASEAN are adequate as at 4Q23, albeit with the caveat of a broader measure of reserves (i.e., include US\$ liquidity in domestic banks).

However, there is a fair degree of variation within the region (Table 1), where the Philippines (3.7x) and Thailand (2.6x) enjoy significantly higher US\$ liquidity buffers than Indonesia (1.4x) and Malaysia (1.1x). Even on a historical basis, US\$ liquidity buffers in the Philippines and Thailand have improved more than Indonesia and Malaysia in recent years (Figure 1).

Figure 1: ASEAN US\$ liquidity buffer
![](images/71852d1efcb7dd80a949504604aa1a9bcbbd46117dccb650df2259b10cee0169.jpg)

The bulk of changes in US\$ liquidity buffers since the start of the post-pandemic tightening in external conditions (from Fed rate hikes and commodity price shock) is in turn, attributed to changes in US\$ liquidity itself (Figure 2), where central bank reserves were used to manage volatility. This also means that ASEAN’s macro fundamentals or external risk profile has not materially changed, as evident in a stable reserve adequacy (RA) requirement (Figure 3).

## Identifying the stress points

Given the variation in economic/BOP structures, it is not surprising that RA levels show compositional differences (Table 2) across ASEAN. For example, exports take up a higher share of RA for trade-dependent economies (e.g., Malaysia, Thailand; Figure 4), while more reserves, percentage wise, are needed to cover a pullback in foreign portfolio debt flows for twin-deficit economies (e.g., Indonesia, Philippines).

The Philippines has the lowest RA in the region but if adjusted by the size of the economy (8% of GDP), it is roughly on par with Indonesia (6% of GDP). Still, Philippines’ RA compares favorably with Thailand (18% of GDP) and Malaysia (23% of GDP), likely because of differences in debt levels and financialization.

Figure 4: IMF Assessing Reserve Adequacy (ARA) metric
![](images/59b9cbae067dc5f39f8dddf17b258142c496a2279ea474aa0c6a923d794cacc1.jpg)

Another key observation is the relatively high RA level for Malaysia, which can be attributed to short-term external debt (8% of GDP, 36% of RA). But the caveat is that the banking sector constitutes an outsized portion of short-term external debt (Figure 5), specifically in intragroup borrowings, reflecting the regional footprint of Malaysian banks. This maybe a mitigating factor, depending on the liquidity, capital adequacy, and risk profile (e.g., FX, duration mismatch) of the Malaysian banking sector.

![](images/37877fb8f0ca9968f6837af669d23598b108d812a5def200dd8ad520e6518a51.jpg)

## Watch out for dollarization

Intra-regional variations and country idiosyncrasies aside, ASEAN is facing a common issue in the dollarization of savings, as seen in the rise of foreign currency deposits (Figure 6) amid record-low interest rate differentials with the US (Figure 7). The active conversion from local currency to foreign currency deposits due to yield searching behavior, if persists, exerts more pressure on central bank reserves, especially on economies with high M2/GDP ratios (e.g., Thailand, Malaysia).

Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan estimates

## Private investment index (Jun 28)

<table><tr><td colspan="5">% change</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>-2.3</td><td>6.3</td><td>2.7</td><td>-3.0</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m, sa</td><td>-0.9</td><td>3.2</td><td>1.2</td><td>-2.9</td></tr></table>

After a strong April print, domestic demand moderated, with private investment (-2.9%m/m, sa; -3.0%oya) slowing. The sharp monthly decline in private investment was driven by capital goods imports and domestic machinery sales, even as construction material sales picked up on the catch-up in fiscal spending.

Domestic demand moderated, with private investment slowing more than private consumption (0.3%m/m, sa; 1.2%oya). The post-pandemic recovery in private consumption is intact, albeit with a wide disconnect between services and durables spending.

## India

• Monsoon delays push up vegetable prices

• June inflation tracking 50 bps higher, close to 5% oya

• Unexpected monsoon underperformance risks pushing out monetary easing

• Current account deficit narrows sharply in FY24

Due to delays in the monsoon (June rainfall 17% below normal), a prolonged heat spell has hurt vegetable production and resulted in price spikes in various parts of the country. Prices have shot up for tomatoes and green vegetables in particular (Figure 1). The weight of vegetables in the CPI basket is not large (6%) but the sheer amplitude of the vegetable price shock means it will impact overall inflation (Figure 2). June CPI inflation, earlier expected to be close to 4.5% oya (after May’s 4.7%), is now expected to be close to 5%. Inflation in the coming months (July and August) was expected to fall sharply due to large base effects from last year’s spike in tomato prices. Now the dip in inflation is likely to be more modest.

Figure 1: Tomato retail prices
![](images/a82885423d7661a7d18d5773ea17e683cde1d70c0eefa9a71e1fcd9561348e3f.jpg)

Figure 2: Food inflation
![](images/ff3e70d7fbcdf8cf5e509ed686c04d2f546b81afd5d6d8b2895a731392b833a0.jpg)
Source: MoSPI, J.P. Morgan

Vegetables are short cycle crops, and price surges are typically short lived (2-3 months). In an empirical study, the RBI found that “historically the tomato has been an important contributor to volatility in overall inflation, and its volatility also gets transmitted into prices of other vegetables”. However, tomatoes are highly perishable items with a very short crop

cycle, and therefore episodes of price surges are typically short-lived. The same study finds that in the last 13 years, the spell of elevated tomato prices typically lasted just 2.6 fortnights (less than 6 weeks) and after the episode, prices tend to reverse back to their trend level. However, in the current episode, reversal in vegetable prices may take longer as monsoons are just around the corner. Historically, vegetable prices go up during the monsoon season as excessive rain or flooding could adversely impact standing crop.

The RBI typically would look through surges emanating from volatile vegetable price inflation. However, this time around, the RBI is already concerned about the frequency of the food price shocks, and another shock would only add to the central bank’s food inflation worries. The unexpected monsoon underperformance risks pushing out monetary easing from 3Q24 to 4Q24.

## FY24 current account narrows sharply

The 1Q24 current account balance (CAB) printed a surplus of US\$6 bn nsa (0.6% of GDP), not far from our expectation of US\$4 bn, and a significant improvement from a deficit of US\$8.7 bn nsa (1% of GDP) in 4Q23. With January-March being the last quarter of the fiscal year, typically there is a large improvement in the current account. In this case, the improvement was led by a jump in exports ex. oil and softer imports ex. oil and gold (Table 1). Going forward, we expect a sharp swing in the current account balance (CAB), from a surplus in 1Q24 to a deficit of \~1.5% of GDP in 2Q24, led by stronger imports and some slowing in net service exports. Net services exports have continued to ease and came at a 11- month low of US\$12.8 bn in May, driven by an increase in services imports even as services exports remained largely similar.

Table 1: Current Account Balance
<table><tr><td>($ billions)</td><td>Jun-23</td><td>Sep-23</td><td>Dec-23</td><td>Mar-24</td></tr><tr><td>Exports</td><td>105</td><td>108</td><td>107</td><td>122</td></tr><tr><td>Imports</td><td>162</td><td>173</td><td>177</td><td>173</td></tr><tr><td>Trade balance</td><td>-57</td><td>-65</td><td>-70</td><td>-51</td></tr><tr><td>Invisibles</td><td>48</td><td>53</td><td>61</td><td>57</td></tr><tr><td>Services</td><td>35</td><td>40</td><td>45</td><td>43</td></tr><tr><td>Transfers</td><td>23</td><td>25</td><td>29</td><td>29</td></tr><tr><td>Net Investment Income</td><td>-10</td><td>-12</td><td>-13</td><td>-15</td></tr><tr><td>Current Account Balance</td><td>-9.0</td><td>-11.3</td><td>-8.7</td><td>5.7</td></tr><tr><td>% of GDP</td><td>-1.0</td><td>-1.3</td><td>-1.0</td><td>0.6</td></tr></table>

Source: RBI, J.P. Morgan

For the year as a whole in FY24, the CAB came at a benign -0.7% of GDP (Figure 3). This is a big improvement from -2.0% of GDP in FY23 despite oil prices remaining high (Brent averaged US\$ 82/bbl from 95/bbl in FY23). This is inline with our view that the current account in India has undergone a structural compression on higher service exports and the economy’s reduced oil intensity. Primarily, the improvement in the current account in FY24 was due to higher service exports and lower imports ex. oil and gold. The latter was partly on the back of a normalization in commodity prices after the Russia-Ukraine conflict.

Figure 3: Current Account Deficit
![](images/06de166c4e2802e08bfca897a81454ff22ae714f77002c9d32079ada74b7140d.jpg)

## Portfolio flows perk up

The capital account printed at US\$86 bn (2.4% of GDP) for FY24 higher than US\$59 bn (1.7% of GDP) last year but closer to the last three years’ average of 2.3% of GDP. However, the composition of the capital flows in FY24 was different with lower FDI and higher portfolio flows (Figure 4). Net FDI flows have fallen sharply to US\$11 bn in FY24, which is slightly lower than even the 14-years’ low of US\$12 bn in FY11. Within FDI, gross FDI has remained unchanged in dollar terms, but the decline has been led by a large jump in repatriation/disinvestments that have depressed “net FDI” flows. Portfolio flows were higher at US\$44 bn in FY24 – the highest ever in absolute terms - after an outflow of US\$5.2 bn in FY23. In FY25, we expect portfolio flows and thereby capital flows to remain strong, in part helped by India’s inclusion in the J.P. Morgan GBI-EM Global Series (bond index).

![](images/228a844baa20d2d0aa96516caf1b3912748466175db331d032024c05a0c79272.jpg)

## Asia Focus: House prices and rental CPI

China’s 70-city new home prices already declined 6.5% as of May compared to the peak in August 2021. But the passthrough to China’s rent CPI has been very limited, down 0.18%oya on average since 2022, compared to the 2.4%oya average gain in the three years prior to the pandemic (Figure 1). Outside of China, most economies in the region experienced a house price upturn during the post-pandemic period, followed by some retreat amid liquidity tightening. This pattern is most evident in Korea, with house price gains as high as 10.6%oya in late 2021 and a steep decline of 8.8% in mid-2023 (Figure 2). While there were also ups and downs in Korea’s rental CPI inflation, it has largely stayed in positive territory though it has become more muted in recent months.

Figure 1: China housing price and rent CPI
![](images/73ffbf996cae845bf46b8a894df9a109106eb27a44419fb0ab79632d1636889b.jpg)

Figure 2: Korea housing price and rent CPI
![](images/27de7562359a66df0041207299b94af858ad91f941552843a5def7dee51354e8.jpg)
Source: CEIC, J.P. Morgan

Japan and Taiwan are different, partly due to their less synchronized policy adjustment with most DM central banks. Ultra-low funding cost (especially in real terms), loose liquidity, and accumulation of household savings during the pandemic contributed to an elevated housing price upturn in Japan, with the gain extending to this year (Figure 3). Though with a transmission lag, Japan’s housing CPI inflation turned up for most of 2021 as well, averaging the full-year at 1.1%oya. That compares to an 0.14%oya average decline in the decade prior to the pandemic.

In Taiwan, after a modest correction in 2023, housing prices surged again this year (Figure 4). That has pushed up a sustained gain in Taiwan’s CPI, more than that implied by the historical linkage between housing prices and rental measures under CPI. It is partly supported by a favorable household income recovery. Worries of a further boom in the housing market has prompted the Taiwan CBC to hike the reserve ratio and tighten credit policy for the housing sector. Another exception in the region is Hong Kong with its prolonged housing price downturn (Figure 5), due to both emigration during 2020 and 2021, and liquidity tightening under the linked exchange rate system despite soft fundamentals.

Figure 3: Japan housing price and rent CPI
![](images/76fa087e39453ffade7b86fa400a46458fbc516b0fda0f59c1f3d375a9f6c5fc.jpg)

Figure 4: Taiwan housing price and rent CPI %oya, both scales
![](images/a24d287df4c7295eddfe776d699bcf1f3e26157461fbb6d0652beca50540d495.jpg)

Figure 5: Hong Kong private housing rentals and housing CPI
![](images/5c7e8de221326153156001967d1bcdf941ebd6aea8368f3716e809425f6aaf4f.jpg)
Source: HKC&SD, J.P. Morgan

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
